Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

6 Berry-Bearing Shrubs to Plant Now and Feed Hungry Birds Through Winter

7 Underrated Perennials to Plant in October – For The Gorgeous Fall Color You Never Knew You Were Missing

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Hidden in plain sight in the posters and trailers, these 12 plants could reveal more than you think about The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

There's something about Suzanne Collins' iconic dystopian series that feels more relevant than ever, and, with the latest film taking us back to the 50th Hunger Games, there's plenty for fans to scrutinize beyond the action itself. Personally, though, I can't stop looking at the plants.

If you've followed the franchise, you'll know that nature has never been merely decorative in Panem. From sinister plants and poisonous berries to the natural world providing food, healing plants, and shelter, knowing your botany in the arena can quite literally mean the difference between life and death.

There's also a long literary tradition of using plants as an act of resistance. After all, flowers have carried hidden messages for centuries, from the symbolism of roses in mythology to the elaborate Victorian language of flowers, when a carefully chosen bouquet could communicate everything from secret affection to heartbreak.

Watch The Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping Trailer:

A post shared by The Hunger Games (@thehungergames)

Even the names of some of the franchise's most memorable characters have botanical connections. Katniss is named after Sagittaria latifolia, a real North American aquatic plant also known as a swamp potato thanks to its edible tubers – making it a rather fitting namesake for a character whose survival instincts are so closely connected to the natural world.

Rue, meanwhile, shares her name with an aromatic herb traditionally associated with regret and sorrow (which is heartbreaking when you consider her story). And it seems the trailers and posters for The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping suggest that the franchise's botanical language is far from finished.

From wildflowers carpeting the arena to carefully arranged floral displays around some of Panem's most recognisable characters, there are plenty of plants worth looking twice at in the posters and trailers alike.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Some have fascinating historical meanings, others have surprisingly sinister botanical properties, and more than a few might offer clues about the story's characters and themes.

With that in mind, then, here are 12 botanical details I've spotted – and what they could mean.

The striking urn plant, Aechmea fasciata, is a popular bromeliad that's hard to miss in the above Sunrise On The Reaping poster thanks to its architectural rosette of silvery-green leaves and bright pink flower spike.

Its name comes from the distinctive cup formed by its overlapping leaves, which collects water in its natural habitat. This adaptation helps the plant make the most of the resources around it, even when growing conditions are challenging.

It's easy to see why it makes an interesting symbol in the Hunger Games universe. Urn plants are associated with resilience, adaptation and protection – qualities that are essential when survival is the ultimate prize. There's something particularly fitting about a plant that creates its own little reservoir of resources in a world where nothing comes easily, right?

Foxgloves (Digitalis purpurea) are among Britain's most recognisable wildflowers. Their tall flower spikes and bell-shaped blooms make them a favourite with pollinators and cottage garden enthusiasts... but there's a darker side to these pretty flowers. Every part of the foxglove plant is poisonous, containing compounds called cardiac glycosides that affect the heart.

Interestingly, those same compounds have played an important role in medicine. Digitalis-derived drugs have been used to treat certain heart conditions, making foxgloves a fascinating example of how the line between medicine and poison can depend on the dose.

In the Hunger Games, then, foxgloves could represent the duality of healing and danger – something that feels particularly at home in an arena where even the most beautiful things can be deadly.

White roses have long been associated with innocence, purity and remembrance. In Victorian flower dictionaries, their meanings included innocence, silence and even a holy death. Their appearance around President Snow is therefore particularly interesting, especially considering he's one of Panem's most ruthless figures.

Could they be a visual reminder of the innocence destroyed by the Capitol? Or perhaps a suggestion that even the most pristine-looking things can conceal something rotten underneath?

If white roses suggest purity, red roses are traditionally associated with passionate love and desire. They have been used for centuries as declarations of affection, but their symbolism isn't always straightforward. In the Victorian language of flowers, different shades and arrangements could carry different messages, from deep devotion to longing.

When red roses appear around President Snow, it's difficult not to consider the contrast between romantic passion and the bloodshed associated with his regime. After all, their vivid color also makes them an interesting counterpart to the white roses. Together, then, the two colors create a visual language of innocence, passion, power and mortality... which is very Hunger Games-coded.

The California poppy (Eschscholzia californica) is instantly recognisable by its bright orange flowers. Native to western North America, it's also the official state flower of California. It's a hardy plant that thrives in sunny conditions and can tolerate relatively poor soils, making it a lovely symbol of resilience.

Poppies have different meanings across cultures, but they're frequently associated with remembrance, sleep and renewal. The California poppy's connection to the American landscape, though, makes it particularly interesting in the world of the Hunger Games, especially as it reimagines a future North America as Panem... especially as its bright and cheerful appearance feels so ironic against the darker world in which the Games take place.

Try the Burpee Sunset Mixed California Poppy Flower Seed from Walmart if you're in the mood for shades of orange, yellow, and cream in your own garden. You can even sow the seeds directly into gravel or cracks, too!

If you've spotted the sunflowers in the character poster for Percy Daggs IV as Ampert, you're not alone in wondering what they mean for the new Hunger Games film. After all, it's difficult to look at a sunflower (Helianthus annuus) without thinking of sunshine, happiness and optimism.

These towering annuals are often associated with hope, loyalty and resilience. Their ability to grow quickly and produce impressive flower heads makes them a familiar symbol of strength and positivity, and there's also a lovely modern interpretation of sunflower symbolism that centers on community and supporting one another through difficult times.

In a story where alliances can mean survival? Well, that sense of solidarity feels particularly poignant.

Canterbury bells (Campanula medium) are charming cottage garden flowers with tall stems covered in nodding bell-shaped blooms. And, fun fact, they're also the birth month biennial for May babies, too!

Their traditional Victorian flower meaning was 'acknowledgement', while other historical dictionaries associated them more generally with constancy. And, in a story about loyalty, friendship and the people we stand alongside when times get difficult, both interpretations feel rather fitting.

Celebrate the Tributes' attempt to break the machine, then, with a packet of Canterbury Bells Double Mix from Amazon.

The unusual pincushion protea, from the genus Leucospermum, has an unmistakable appearance. Its spiky flower heads look almost like elaborate fireworks or, as the name suggests, a pincushion.

Native to southern Africa, these evergreen shrubs are adapted to challenging growing conditions and are known for their striking flowers. Proteas more broadly, though, have become associated with transformation, strength, courage and diversity. As such, their unusual appearance also makes them a natural symbol of individuality.

Their presence around Effie Trinket is a possible nod to her flamboyant personality, as Effie has always been someone who embraces color, extravagance and standing out from the crowd, even when the world around her is anything but cheerful. Peersonally, though, this writer thinks it might be a nod to her eventual character arc...

Toadstools might not technically be plants – they're fungi, as we made very clear in our recent piece on birth month mushrooms – but they deserve a place in this botanical spotter's guide.

Fungi are essential to healthy ecosystems, breaking down dead organic matter and recycling nutrients back into the soil. Some species, however, are highly poisonous, while others have medicinal or mind-altering properties, and that contrast gives mushrooms a fascinating symbolic role. They can represent decay and death, but also renewal, transformation and altered states of consciousness.

In the world of the Hunger Games, where the natural environment is often manipulated to confuse, frighten or kill its inhabitants? I suspect toadstools are a fitting reminder that not everything growing in the wild should be trusted.

Bee balm (Monarda) is a North American perennial loved by bees, butterflies and hummingbirds. Its colorful and nectar-rich flowers (particularly this stunning Pocahontas Purple Rose Bee Balm from Nature Hills) make it a wonderful addition to pollinator-friendly gardens, while the plant also has a history of traditional herbal use.

Today, bee balm is sometimes associated with compassion, empathy and comfort during difficult times. An RHS study of historical flower dictionaries, however, reveals that one nineteenth-century interpretation of this super-spreader was rather less affectionate: "Your whims are quite unbearable".

That's quite a contrast to the modern associations of care and kindness – and makes its appearance in the sinister world of the Hunger Games all the more intriguing... especially for those who remember the role they played in the Sunrise On The Reaping book.

Now here's a botanical detail I particularly love, as Nepenthes is a genus of tropical carnivorous plants, commonly known as tropical pitcher plants. Their distinctive modified leaves form hanging pitchers filled with digestive fluid, which trap and break down insects and other small prey.

The genus name has a fascinating origin. It comes from the Greek word nepenthes, associated with a mythical substance that banished sorrow or grief, which is an extraordinary name for a plant that catches and digests its victims.

In the context of the Hunger Games, the contrast is almost too good to ignore. A plant whose name evokes freedom from grief, but whose survival strategy involves trapping and consuming other creatures? Surely that's a dark metaphor for Panem itself (and almost worth buying a purple pitcher plant from the Ragnaroc Storein the run-up to the film, too).

A post shared by The Hunger Games (@thehungergames)

Spanish bluebells (Hyacinthoides hispanica) are popular spring-flowering bulbs, producing upright stems covered in clusters of blue, pink or white bell-shaped flowers. Unlike native blooms, though, these can spread vigorously in gardens and hybridize with native bluebells – something which hhis has raised concerns about the genetic integrity of native woodland populations.

The resulting hybrids can be difficult to identify, and experts advise gardeners living close to native bluebell populations to avoid planting Spanish bluebells. In terms of the Hunger Games, then, it's an interesting possible metaphor for displacement, hybridization and the arrival of an outsider that changes an established ecosystem.

In a story so concerned with power, territory and the destruction of existing communities, it feels like an idea well worth considering.

Beyond the individual plants seen across the posters and trailers for The Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping, it's the wider meadow-like landscape of the arena that deserves attention.

Wildflowers are usually associated with freedom, biodiversity and the natural world growing without human interference, and tend to evoke a sense of peace and escape from the pressures of modern life. This arena, though, turns that idea on its head, forcing nature to become a part of the spectacle. Its beauty is carefully presented, but the environment is ultimately controlled by the Gamemakers. A field of flowers might look like an idyllic meadow, yet it exists within a space designed to test, manipulate and endanger the tributes.

It's a clever contrast between the natural world as something beautiful and life-giving, and nature as something that can be manipulated into a weapon. And perhaps that's the biggest botanical message of all: in Panem, you should never assume that anything is harmless. Even something as simple and innocuous as, say, a flower.

Kayleigh is an enthusiastic (sometimes too enthusiastic!) gardener and has worked in media for over a decade. She previously served as digital editor at Stylist magazine, and has written extensively for Ideal Home, Woman & Home, Homes & Gardens, and a handful of other titles. Kayleigh is passionate about wildlife-friendly gardening, and recently cancelled her weekend plans to build a mini pond when her toddler found a frog living in their water barrel. As such, her garden – designed around the stunning magnolia tree at its centre – is filled to the brim with pollinator-friendly blooms, homemade bird feeders, and old logs for insects to nest in.

Jürgen Klopp addresses controversial comments surrounding Niclas Füllkrug recall: “I’m obviously not thrilled with those statements.”

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Germany national team head coach Jürgen Klopp has explained his decision to recall Werder Bremen striker Niclas Füllkrug for the upcoming Nations League fixtures against Serbia and the Netherlands. The Bundestrainer also addressed his recent comments about the 33-year-old’s international prospects, revealing that the two had spoken personally well before Thursday’s squad announcement.

Füllkrug returns to the national team for the first time in over a year following an extraordinary start to the 2026/27 Bundesliga campaign. The veteran has scored nine goals in eight league appearances since returning to Bremen, forcing his way back into Germany’s plans despite earlier questions about his age.

As previously reported by Get German Football News, Klopp discussed Füllkrug’s international prospects during his halftime appearance on DAZN‘s broadcast of Bayern Munich’s Champions League encounter with Arsenal in October.

The former Liverpool coach acknowledged the striker’s excellent form but emphasized that Germany’s preparations for Euro 2028 required consideration of both immediate and longer-term objectives. Klopp’s subsequent decision to include Füllkrug in his 24-man November squad has now prompted him to clarify those remarks.

Speaking in a video interview distributed by the DFB, Klopp emphasized that Füllkrug’s influence at Bremen extends well beyond his impressive goalscoring record.

“Good form and scoring goals are never a bad thing,” Klopp explained. “Fülle has been performing and scoring all kinds of goals, almost all of them spectacular. He has an incredible influence on Werder Bremen, not just through his goals, but also through the way he carries himself.”

Klopp also acknowledged that he was not entirely pleased with some of his previous television comments, although he maintained that his earlier remarks about Füllkrug had been factual. Klopp found it necessary to clear the air with the striker himself before assembling his latest squad.

“We’d talked about each other so much that I thought we might as well talk to each other,” Klopp explained. “I’m obviously not thrilled with the statements I made in that half-time interview, but what I said about Fülle were just facts.”

Klopp also made clear that the striker’s age would not prevent him from earning an international place if his current form warranted selection.

“If the European Championship were tomorrow, we’d naturally take him. There’s no question about that,” Klopp said. “From that perspective, the nomination was relatively easy. It was a pleasure to make the decision and a pleasure to tell him.”

Füllkrug, who has scored 14 goals in 24 senior international appearances, welcomed the opportunity to represent Germany again.

“I’m delighted about the nomination,” the Bremen striker said in comments released by his club. “I’ve benefited from the good performances of our team, and I see it as wonderful recognition for the entire squad.”

Even When Everything Goes Right, the Backcountry Can Turn Fatal. What College Recreation Clubs Are Doing About It.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Search-and-rescue responders with the Chaffee County Sheriff's Office search for two missing Colorado ice climbers (Photo: Courtesy of CCSO)

The small Colorado Mountain College community in Leadville is in mourning after two of its students—19-year-old Kaden Matsuda and 20-year-old Jackson Donlon—died in late October when their climbing anchor failed during their descent of West Apostle Mountain.

While investigators emphasized that the pair took extensive safety precautions, their deaths come amid a string of recent high-profile outdoor accidents involving college students.

A 21-year-old with the University of Cincinnati’s Mountaineering Club was found dead on October 12 after he slipped while crossing a stream during a trip in West Virginia. A 19-year-old wrestler at a California community college also fell to his death while hiking on September 20 in Yosemite National Park. In Kentucky, nine college students from Indiana and Illinois called search-and-rescue officials after they became stranded on a trail called Indian Staircase on August 15. That same month, four college students from New Jersey were rescued in New Hampshire after departing late and getting caught by nightfall. In January, a helicopter rescued three college-aged hikers from Utah’s Mount Olympus.

Andy Millick owns Sharp End Rescue, a search-and-rescue training company in northern Vermont. He told Outside that many outdoor hobbies have a learning curve—from mastering basics like what to bring or how to pack a backpack to developing map-reading and weather-monitoring skills.

“A lot of people grow up in a world that doesn’t have access to those experiences,” he said.

Younger people sometimes venture into the backcountry without adequate preparation, the proper equipment, or familiarity with the terrain, he said. They may also lack the experience and judgment to recognize and avoid unnecessary risks. As Millick noted, they don’t always fully understand the prep work that it will require.

“There’s [sometimes] a miss in all the preparation it takes to really have fun,” he said. “That’s where people start getting into trouble.”

On October 18, Millick helped rescue a 19-year-old hypothermic college student in the middle of the night on a remote section of the Long Trail in Vermont. The hiker was on a student-led club outing, and when trouble hit, the group acted quickly.

“[They] got him in a sleeping bag with more layers, got another student in that sleeping bag as well to share some body heat, put sleeping pads underneath him to keep him off the ground,” Millick said.

“[They did] all the right things at that point,” Millick said. “Unfortunately, the tricky thing with hypothermia is once somebody starts to get cold, it takes a long time to get them warm again.”

The group contacted a faculty advisor for additional help and called 911 as the sick student went in and out of consciousness. When first responders arrived in the early morning hours, they continued warming the student with additional measures, including a heated blanket. By morning, the student had improved enough to walk out with the rescue team’s help.

Millick said it made a huge difference that the trip leader had a Wilderness First Responder certification, an 80-hour course that teaches backcountry survival skills.

“They learned so much more coming away from that and utilizing the skills that they had been taught and seeing, ‘Hey, this stuff is real, and it does work,’” he said.

College outdoor recreation clubs expose young people to the outdoors and usually center on activities such as hiking, backpacking, or adventure sports like kayaking. Some of these, such as the hiking club at the University of Colorado, Boulder, and the Dartmouth Outing Club, are more than 100 years old. Historically, many of these groups are student-run, organized, and often have a faculty advisor.

“It’s an opportunity to introduce people to outdoor programming that maybe they’ve never been introduced to [and] to step outside their comfort zone,” Jan Hackett, who ran outdoor programs at Duke University in the late ‘90s and early 2000s, told Outside.

Hackett now owns Wilderness Education and Medicine of Texas, a company that offers wilderness education classes. Outdoor clubs let more experienced students build the skills needed to lead an outing. Proper training, Hackett says, is imperative to that leadership. At a minimum, Hackett believes a trip leader should be required to have wilderness first aid certification, which teaches individuals how to handle serious injuries or heat- and cold-related emergencies, including hypothermia.

“The instructor is probably going to mix in some stories–some real disasters–that happen to drive home the point: this is why we do this,” Hackett told Outside. “It’s about risk management, having you prepared in the event a disaster happens.”

The goal of this training is not to scare students or dampen their enthusiasm, he says, but to teach that assessing risks can help combat any feelings of invincibility that may accompany youth.

“Safety is your judgment,” Hackett said. “Even if they are 22 years old, sometimes they haven’t had as much experience, so how do [they] get some of that judgment? It’s through training.”

Zach Waksmonski, 19, is the president of the student-run Penn State Outing Club at State College, Pennsylvania. A college junior, Waksmonski told Outside that his group helps build a sense of community around a shared passion for hiking and backpacking.

It’s also taught him to be more vigilant about anticipating the unexpected.

“Even if it says that there’s no chance of rain for a given weekend, if you’re going on a backpacking trip, you’re definitely still going to want to have some sort of rain protection,” he said.

Penn State temporarily shut down the program in 2018 because it deemed it too risky. After the school faced a backlash, the club was reinstated with new safety guidelines. Now trips require a certain number of students to either be Wilderness First Aid or responder certified.

Waksmonski says some members miss activities that they could have done in the past, such as climbing expeditions. But he said the standardized tools now in place help set students up for success. For example, participants must fill out a detailed planning document that lists how they’ll manage any potential hazards, and it undergoes peer review.

“Those give us a framework where if something unexpected arises, we can more effectively deal with it,” Waksmonski said. “It goes from being a day-ruining event to a minor inconvenience.”

Students also submit post-trip reports where they can identify teachable moments. One recent example? A student wore shorts on a trail with overgrown, thorny vegetation. The takeaway: pants are advisable.

Safety and enjoyment in the outdoors come down to proper preparation. Millick advises groups to check the functionality of any equipment they’re using before setting out and to understand the terrain and weather in the area they’re targeting.

“Stuff absolutely happens, [but] it’s how you react to it in the moment,” he said.

Always tell other people about your plans, he adds, and assess the group’s knowledge base, including map-reading skills and first-aid capabilities. Millick also says to thoroughly run through what-if scenarios and develop contingency plans for being out longer than expected.

“If we run out of water, what are we going to do for a water source? Do we have the ability to treat or filter water? Millick said. “Do we have multiple ways to build a fire or heat source?”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/cigna-beats-street-14b-profit-737b-revenue-q3" hreflang="en">Cigna offers investors a look at its new Lead to One strategy</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →
Cigna offers investors a look at its new Lead to One strategy" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Cigna offered investors additional color on its new healthcare transformation strategy on Thursday morning.

First unveiled at the company's investor day on Sept. 30, the Lead to One initiative aims to bring together the array of assets under the Cigna Group's umbrella—from medical and pharmacy benefits to behavioral health to specialty care—to address the individual needs of members and patients.

CEO Brian Evanko said during Cigna's earnings call that the program aims to lean on data analytics and technology to identify risks more quickly and deliver personalized supports. The goal, he said, is to drive better outcomes while lowering healthcare costs in tandem.

"Our Lead to One vision will benefit all customers, and we see a particular opportunity with the most clinically complex patient needs, where healthcare costs are increasingly concentrated and represent over 50% of total healthcare spending," he said.

Evanko said that the approach helps to establish "clear strategic growth framework for creating long-term value."

"It is an exciting time here at The Cigna Group as our company unifies around a single vision, Lead to One, and begins to compound the distinctive capabilities we have developed to capture the opportunity set in front of us," he said.

In addition, the company is undertaking an initiative called Accelerate to One, which aims to support operational efficiency for its workforce while supporting greater accountability and better decision-making. Evanko said that program is set to drive $3 billion in run rate savings for the company by 2030.

Evanko said the company expects to see those savings opportunities materialize across the company.

Cigna is the latest health insurance company to boost its guidance for the year on the back of its Q3 results.

The company released its earnings report Thursday morning, posting $1.4 billion in profit. That's down year over year from the $1.9 billion haul the company reported in the third quarter of 2025.

In addition, revenues for the quarter were $73.7 billion, up from $69.7 billion in the prior-year quarter. Both figures surpassed Wall Street analysts' predictions, per Zacks Investment Research.

Through the first three quarters of the year, the company has earned $4.8 billion in profit, on par with the $4.7 billion posted for the first nine months of 2025. Total revenue at the end of Q3 was $213.8 billion, growing from $202.4 billion through three quarters in 2025.

CEO Brian Evanko said in the release that the company's current financial strategy is rooted in three key principles, including "driving core growth, leveraging our distinct capabilities and executing with discipline."

"As we put our strategy in action, our strong third quarter results demonstrate how we are building on this momentum by making health care more affordable and personalized, while advancing Accelerate to One to drive productivity, reduce costs, and support sustained long-term earnings growth," he said.

The company reported a medical loss ratio in the quarter of 85%, up slightly from 84.8% in Q3 2025. Cigna attributed the uptick to higher costs in its employer plans, while lower costs in the individual market segment did partially offset this trend.

Cigna Healthcare, the company's core insurance business, reported 18.45 million members as of Q3. By comparison, the insurer had 18.1 million members at the end of 2025.

Pharmacy benefit membership, meanwhile, was 117.7 million, down 123.6 million as of Dec. 31, 2025. Cigna said the decrease reflects "expected client transitions" as well as lower membership at its health plan clients.

Its Evernorth unit reported $63.8 billion in adjusted revenue for the quarter, up from $61.5 billion a year ago. Highlights include ongoing organic growth in its specialty pharmacy business, the company said.

Cigna Healthcare reported $11.7 billion in adjusted revenue for Q3, up from $10.7 billion in the prior-year quarter. The company said this largely reflects higher premium revenue as it adapts to higher medical costs.

On the back of the results, Cigna elevated its outlook and now expects at least $30.60 in earnings per share for 2026. It also projects a full-year MLR of 84.2%.

Wendy's rejects $1.4 billion takeover proposal from Nelson Peltz’s Trian

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Wendy's on Thursday said it is rejecting a $1.4 billion takeover offer from a group of investors led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Fund Management.

The group offered $7.35 per share in cash on Oct. 28 to take the struggling burger chain private. In addition to Trian, it included investment firms BlueFive Capital and Sixth Street Partners, plus Flynn Group, one of Wendy's largest franchisees.

In February, Wendy's board formed a committee of independent directors to assess any potential bid from Trian in response to a regulatory filing from the firm. The committee found that the proposal "substantially undervalues" Wendy's and does not reflect potential gains from the chain's ongoing turnaround strategy, the company said in a press release Thursday.

"Our confidence rests firmly in [CEO] Bob Wright's proven track record of seasoned industry leadership, including restaurant business turnarounds," Art Winkleblack, Wendy's board chair and a member of the independent committee, said in a statement.

Trian declined to comment on Wendy's decision.

To stave off a hostile takeover, Wendy's has adopted a shareholder rights plan — or poison pill — with a one-year duration, as of Wednesday, the company said. Wendy's added that the investor group led by Trian rejected an offer from Wendy's for a standstill agreement that would have paused additional share purchases, adding to Wendy's decision to use a poison pill.

The rejection is the latest in the long-running saga between Peltz and the burger chain, which dates back to an activist campaign he led more than two decades ago. A Peltz firm has even owned Wendy's before. In 2008, his company Triarc, which also owned Arby's, bought Wendy's in a $2.3 billion deal. It later sold Arby's, turning Wendy's into a stand-alone company again.

In 2024, Wendy's named Peltz as chairman emeritus after he spent 17 years on the company's board. Trian executive Peter May and Peltz's son, Bradley, still sit on Wendy's board.

Wendy's also reported its third-quarter results on Thursday. Its same-store sales declined for the seventh straight quarter. However, its quarterly earnings and revenue topped Wall Street's expectations.

Shares of Wendy's fell less than 1% in morning trading. The takeover offer had lifted the stock, which has lost about 60% of its value over the last three years.

Wendy's is not the only restaurant chain that has found itself in the crosshairs of activist investors. Applebee's owner Dine Brands recently adopted a poison pill to block a takeover bid from investment firms Arkhouse Management and Cobblers Cove Capital.

What’s Your Hiking EDC? Backpacker’s Editors Share Their ‘Every Day Carry’ Gear

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Associate Editor Will Brendza's EDC requires three to four pockets, but prepares him with tools, fire-starter, light, and his favorite pair of sunglasses. (Photo: Will Brendza)

It seems like everywhere online people are sharing photos of their “Every Day Carry” (EDC) kits. Watches, pocket knives, key fobs, and other day-to-day, on-your-person gear are photographed in a spread and posted to socials with a quippy caption. Often, it’s tactical bros who just want to show off their knives and handguns. For others, it’s an opportunity to flash a Rolex or humble brag about their expensive whip. It’s an internet trend that’s exploded among gear nerds, collectors, survivalists, and preppers.

But what about hikers? We’ve all got a dedicated list of gear we wouldn’t hit the trail without. How would the EDC trend look if you shook people down on the trail to find out what was in their pockets?

We wanted to know. So we’re starting with Backpacker’s editors—to see what our trail EDC looks like and how it’s different from person to person. We’re curious what others’ might look like, too. Tag @backpackermag in a photo of your hiker EDC on Instagram, and we’ll check it out.

Here’s what our team is never leaving the house without. #hikerEDC

The most important piece of gear I keep on me is my Ombraz Armless Sunglasses. I’ve got sensitive eyes and a penchant for losing things—these dandies solve both of those problems. They stay tethered to my neck or head, even when I’m underwater or upside down, and offer fully polarized eye protection. Next is my Knives of Alaska folding knife, which is big enough for most camp tasks and still fits in a pocket. I also always keep a bandana handy, because it’s the single most versatile tool a backpacker can carry (serving as a headband, handkerchief, surface cleaner, hot mitt, face mask, etc.).

If you follow Backpacker’s Editor’s Gear Picks, you’ll recognize my Nocs Zero Tube Monocular, which is one of my favorite backpacking tools. For scoping routes, spotting wildlife, or just enhancing the details of a view, it’s an incredible companion. My no-frills Petzl headlamp is getting old, but still serves me well when I get caught on the trail after dark.

Then I usually keep tissues for when I get sniffly, a pen and paper for taking notes, my ID and money, the sunscreen and chapstick I apply on the go, and, of course, a lighter (preferably one with a recognizable graphic so people are less likely to “accidentally” snatch it).

Not pictured: My dog, a compass, and my Coros Pace 4 Pro watch.

I’m on a never-ending quest to see how many essential items I can cram into a waist pack, but one thing I usually leave at home during non-working hours is my smartphone; I’ve found that the more I disconnect, the happier I am. Instead, I’m generally packing an AT&T Cingular Flex 2 flip phone, which cost me all of $15 and, besides texting and calling, can display rudimentary hiking maps with the O.map app. I supplement it with a button compass, which is enough for me to rough out directions, and my Life Camera S168, a tiny digital camera capable of taking better photos and videos than my Walmart phone.

A cheap space blanket has saved my bacon at least once, and I usually add a lighter wrapped in a twist of duct tape and a travel-size tube of sunscreen. My (sadly discontinued) Leatherman Tread multitool bracelet keeps a TSA- and security-friendly cutting tool and a host of screwdriver heads at hand, and for dayhikes where the possibility of being caught after dark is remote, I toss in my (also sadly discontinued) 40-lumen Black Diamond Ion Keychain Light. My wallet-size first aid kit has antibiotic ointment and a couple of adhesive bandages in kid-friendly patterns for the inevitable skinned knees and elbows that happen on family hikes.

I carry a waterproof notepad for jotting things down. My pen of choice is a Pilot Precise Varsity disposable fountain pen; it’s probably not the lightest scribbler out there, but it’s fun to write with. I usually bring cheap sunglasses so I don’t stress when I break them—these are from last year’s Outside Days in Denver. My choice of snacks is cheap, too: fruit snacks, granola bars, or whatever I most recently bought in bulk for my kids. And the toothbrush? I’ve just found my quality of life is way higher if I can brush my teeth whenever I want.

Most days, I walk out my front door and hit my local trails with very little in my pockets save for my iPhone and one or two dog poop bags (whatever brand was on sale last time I ran out). If I’m carrying any snacks, gummy candy is the first thing I reach for, and Nerds Gummy Clusters have never let me down. On long, difficult efforts, I like Honey Stinger Organic Energy Chews. My Garmin Instinct Solar watch is always on my wrist to track mileage or elevation gain, and of course, if I’m going far, I’ll pack my inReach to contact help in an emergency. Whether I’m hitting the trail or exploring a new city, my Nalgene is along for the ride, too.

Not pictured: My Kula cloth, avalanche transceiver for winter adventures, collapsible dog bowl, and some Leukotape wrapped around my sunscreen stick.

Lexi Thompson Is Ready to Drive Beyond Golf’s Old-School Image

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

Lexi Thompson has spent a large portion of her life playing a sport that rewards patience, control, and the ability to keep emotions from interfering with execution. Her next competitive endeavor is going to ask her to show a little more of what she normally conceals between shots.

WTGL, the new women’s team golf league from TMRW Sports and the LPGA, launches this fall (Nov. 8) inside the tech-infused SoFi Center in Palm Beach Gardens, FL. Thompson will represent Motor City Golf Club.

The decision to participate didn’t require much deliberation on Thompson’s part. Living only about 30 minutes from SoFi gave her an opportunity to experience TGL firsthand, and what stood out most wasn’t the technology or the arena setting. It was how much more personality the format allowed fans to see.

“It’s something different, and it brings out a different fan base that we need as well,” Thompson said. “A lot of people don’t get to see our personalities. They only get to see what we do on the golf course.”

The timing also finds Thompson in a different space personally and physically. She is expecting her first child, and while training remains part of nearly every day, her approach has continued evolving from the heavier lifting she once gravitated toward to something much more deliberate.

After years of professional golf, Thompson’s focus is no longer proving what she can do in the weight room. She’s more interested in making sure the work she does there continues to allow her to feel powerful, mobile, healthy, and always ready for whatever comes next.

Professional golf can seem like an isolating exercise in responsibility. There is a caddie alongside you, but the number on the card belongs to only one person, and Thompson has lived with that reality since becoming a pro at just 15.

Team golf changes provides a different dynamic.

Thompson has experience in this arena from the Solheim Cups, International Crowns, and Olympic competition. Another exciting element for Thompson about the WTGL is the chance to make decisions with other players rather than spending an entire competition competing against her own thoughts.

“I love team events in general,” she said. “Golf is so individual, so to be able to have teammates and talk strategy behind each and every shot, I think that’s what also is so great about this.”

In WTGL, players are mic’d up. There are also real-time analytics and shot-clock pressure, which doesn’t sound as daunting to a pro golfer as one would think.

Traditional golf gives a player plenty of time to gather information, reconsider an approach, and allows one thought to become several. A clock makes every decision count.

“The shot clock is definitely something that’s very different, and I actually think it would be good in normal golf, too,” Thompson said. “Sometimes we don’t really think about how much time we’re spending on each and every shot.”

The format should also reveal a version of Thompson that traditional tournament broadcasts rarely capture. She describes herself as more sarcastic and bubbly around people who know her well and credits growing up with two older brothers for developing another useful skill.

“I’ve learned to trash talk quite a bit,” she said. “We’ll see if that gets brought out here, but I think it’s just great for the fans to see all our personalities.”

Fitness wasn’t always a major component of Thompson’s routine.

It took the first couple of years for her to notice something every athlete eventually has to confront: Talent doesn’t prevent fatigue.

She would get tired during rounds, so she hired a trainer and began taking conditioning more seriously. What started as a solution quickly became something she found enjoyment in.

“I got addicted to it,” Thompson said. “I love fitness and working out, and I plan my day around getting at least 30-45 minutes, if not longer, of a workout in.”

Her workouts look a little different at this current moment as she prepares for the birth of her first child, but movement remains a daily priority. Thompson still gravitates toward spinning, incline walking, core work, flexibility, and resistance training, with the emphasis now geared toward lighter weights and higher repetitions.

Years of golf have taught her strength has to serve the swing. Thompson’s power is one of the calling cards of her game, but generating clubhead speed isn’t just a matter of becoming all around stronger.

“A lot of the golf swing power is from the core and lower body, so making sure that I do a lot of the exercises that build and strengthen that,” Thompson said. “But at the same time, you don’t want to lose the flexibility.”

That is why her training includes lots of band work and movements that more closely resemble the rotational and explosive aspects of a golf swing. Speed and technique are a priority within these movements, as she wants the strength to transfer to what she does with a club.

But this wasn’t always her mindset. Just a few years ago, Thompson went through phases where lifting more became its own challenge.

“I probably lifted a lot more weights than I do now,” she said. “I went through spans where I was like, ‘Oh, I’m going to try to hit the highest weight of my life, and it was just like, ‘I don’t know really why I’m doing this.’”

These days, cardio, core work, resistance training, and swing-specific exercises are what she prioritizes, and not the weight on the bar. That evolution is partly due to becoming more seasoned and self-aware. Thompson says the longer she has trained, the better she has become at identifying when something isn’t functioning the way it should.

Earlier in her fitness journey, tests from trainers helped reveal weaknesses in her body she didn’t know existed. Now, she can often recognize  those signals herself.

“My body tells me a lot more,” Thompson said. “’Oh, this is weaker. I’m not seeing this part of my body firing,’ or like, ‘This is sore. This is hurting. Something’s lacking.”

If you had to name a sport that requires extreme athleticism, it’s unlikely golf will be at the forefront of your mind.

From the couch, the sport can seem leisurely. What the television doesn’t convey is all the work that goes into what we see come tournament time.

“I think the normal viewer doesn’t realize how hard it is day in, day out,” Thompson said. “It’s a lot of time out practicing, a lot of hitting balls, rotating, putting our lower body and our backs through a lot of twisting and turning.”

She also made sure to mention another element that is often overlooked.

“Walking seven miles of a golf course on top of that,” she said. “Being in the Florida heat, going out there and practicing three to five hours a day. It’s pretty brutal in the summer.”

Those repetitions make recovery extremely important. Thompson’s approach isn’t built around a reliance on recovery technologies. She typically stretches and foam rolls after workouts, gets an occasional massage, and focuses on making sure she is ready to go again the following day.

Sleep also has become a critical priority. There was a time when Thompson believed four hours was what she needed to function. With years of competition under her belt, she now aims for a minimum of seven.

“Sometimes it’s just hard to really shut the brain off and get a good night’s sleep, especially with the quick turnarounds with tee times and everything,” she said. “But I think it’s a huge priority because then your mind doesn’t function the way you need it to, and neither does your body.”

Thompson also incorporates activation and breathing work before rounds. The exercises prepare the muscles she needs for her swing, while meditation helps settle her into the right mental state before competition.

Post-round recovery works in the opposite direction.

“Being able to shut down your body and being able to let it recover is huge, because you don’t realize what you’re putting your body through for that amount of hours,” Thompson said. “Meditation doesn’t hurt after, just to shut off the mind. That’s a part of the performance as well.”

Thompson’s perspective on fitness has changed over the past several years. Working out has moved past PR’s and physique gains. Today, the golfer says the measure has become more personal and more useful in helping her be ready for the next workout.

“Of course, waking up not having my body hurt helps,” Thompson joked. “Being out here for so many years and golfing since I was five, you feel it a little but more.”

As she trains for the 2028 Olympics, the popular athlete continues juggling a host of opportunities outside of sports.

The US Midterm Election Results Remind Us To Focus On The “Foundation Of Our Democracy”

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The country feels different as the US midterm election results come rolling in. When all of the votes are counted, Democrats are set to win the national popular vote in all House races by about 13 percentage points. The voters have spoken in huge numbers: they want leaders who make everyday life a little bit easier.

Now the politicians must answer, and, essentially, they need to design policies that lower costs for ordinary people in the US. It’s not about revenge: revenge is too distinct from US consumers’ lived experiences.

Paul Krugman, the Nobel Prize-winning economist, says that one of the main things “Democrats need to do now is hold not just Trump’s officials, but also his enablers, accountable.” Representative Robert Garcia of California posted on social media in the same vein: “Lawyer Up” to the MAGA losers.

No, that’s playground antics. Instead, the next two years must demonstrate a willingness to listen to US citizens as each family struggles to make ends meet with groceries, household utilities, healthcare, and other daily costs.

Roy Cooper’s Senate campaign in North Carolina had a mantra that permeated every stop on his statewide tour: Make Stuff Cost Less.

James Talarico, Jon Ossoff, Roy Cooper, among others, grounded their campaigns in economic populist agendas. They harangued against President Trump’s economic record and called out corruption for what it is — self-promotion over the interest of US citizens.

Georgia’s Senator-elect Jon Ossoff made Trump’s assault on the rule of law practical. In everyday terms he pointed out to voters how democracy and economic distress depend on each other. He argued that “corruption is why nothing works for ordinary people.”

We are ready as a nation to reintroduce the importance of science to accomplish these goals.

In a recent commentary in the journal, Nature, a group of authors argue that “science is an essential backbone of democracy.” Science as a source of discovery, expertise, and innovation is intertwined, they continue, with democracy as a set of institutions, rules, and procedures. They put forward three pillars through which science enables democracy:

Citizens have legitimate concerns, the scientists agree, “about economic restructuring, regional inequities, how migration is governed, technological changes, ageing populations, climate risks and geopolitical instability.” Then again, “expertise strengthens democratic legitimacy when it is transparent, diverse, independent and open to scrutiny — as prescribed by the rules of science.”

Democracy — when it lives up to its responsibility as the rule of law — contributes to a decrease in CO2 emissions. Climate change, like voting in a democracy, is a collective action problem — a conflict between the individual interest and the group interest.

Congress can look ahead and back science-based clean energy in a variety of ways, from policies to investment, all of which will address the affordability crisis, improve public health, and protect the environment, says Gretchen Colman, president of the Union of Concerned Scientists. That will include renewables and clean transportation, modernizing the power grid, protecting our air and water, and more.

Science-based legislating, Colman continues, must be based “on facts and evidence, not political whims. We’ll petition Congress to restore funding for science, protect federal science agencies, and re-establish safeguards for federal scientists to carry out their work and communicate about it free from political interference.”

A national return to scientific reasoning is underway. Gina Hinojosa, elected as the first Texas Democratic governor, has called for an immediate moratorium on data center development. She has accused developers of “raising our utility rates and depleting our water.” As reported on Bloomberg Green, she’s vowed to halt approvals until “a legislative session that puts Texans first so that we don’t have any more communities in Texas who are powerless against these global corporations.”

Indeed, the output of data centers must also be questioned. Few AI-generated findings are peer-reviewed or tested in laboratories. Talk about mixed messages! We return to a nostalgic past by foregrounding large language models that are programmed to present particular, narrow, and self-serving points of view — generally by billionaires seeking to protect their wealth. It’s time to tax these rare individuals accordingly, eliminate any incentives for their built structures, and require them to provide major renewable energy contributions to communities that house them.

Yes, energy plays an increasingly important role in today’s world. Instead of treating electrification as an option to move us toward decarbonization, it’s time to see it is essential — we need to electrify everything quickly. In the US, more than 60 state and local governments are similarly in court to seek damages from oil companies. These are the lawsuits that Democrats in Congress should be pursuing as complements to transitioning virtually all building, transportation, and industrial systems to electric. This means subsidizing heat pumps and EVs and solar panels and home battery storage — subsidies are far cheaper than funding wars for oil.

The ground-breaking election events have hoisted non-traditional candidates to levels of power and influence, as the New York Times chronicles:

Political analyst Chris Armitrage suggests another important positive trend. “The thing that lasts past January 2027 is the voter who had never voted in a midterm, did it in 2026, and now has the habit,” he concludes.

The urgency of climate change has brought democracy to a critical moment in time. Whether longtime, non-traditional, or new voters, we must continue to argue for inclusive and participatory democracy to address climate change. Shaped by democratic governance, a robust civil society, and innovative advocacy strategies, collective climate action can be a force for the right to a healthy planet.

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a writer, researcher, and educator with a lifelong dedication to ecojustice. Germany November 2026 squad | Five talking points from Jürgen Klopp’s latest selection

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Germany head coach Jürgen Klopp has named his 24-man squad for the upcoming UEFA Nations League fixtures against Serbia and the Netherlands. The selection marks a significant departure from the extraordinary 44-player group assembled during the September and October international window, with the Bundestrainer now beginning to narrow his options.

Germany face Serbia in Belgrade on November 13 before hosting the Netherlands in Berlin three days later. Get German Football News examines the five most important talking points emerging from Klopp’s latest selection.

Naturally, the most prominent returnee is Werder Bremen striker Niclas Füllkrug. The 33-year-old has forced his way back into the national-team picture following an extraordinary opening to the 2026/27 Bundesliga campaign. Nine goals in eight league appearances have established Füllkrug as Germany’s leading domestic goalscorer, providing a compelling argument for his inclusion despite Klopp’s earlier reservations about his age.

Füllkrug’s international prospects appeared considerably less promising only a few months ago. Following difficult spells with West Ham United and AC Milan, the veteran returned to Werder Bremen during the summer in an attempt to revive his career. Füllkrug himself notably grew tired of being asked about a German national team return, but will surely revel in his chance to once again feature for his country.

His performances have also prompted Klopp to reconsider a player who last received a Germany call-up in September 2025. Füllkrug’s return supplies Germany with an established penalty-area striker whose qualities differ from those of Kai Havertz and Younes Ebnoutalib. The Bremen forward’s aerial strength, hold-up play, and instinctive finishing could become particularly useful against opponents prepared to defend deeply.

With Germany having scored only three goals in their opening four Nations League matches, Füllkrug’s return arrives at an opportune moment.

Borussia Mönchengladbach attacking midfielder Wael Mohya is the only player in Klopp’s November squad who has never previously received a senior call-up. The 17-year-old has enjoyed a remarkable emergence at Gladbach, registering four Bundesliga goals in 23 appearances before his 18th birthday in December.

Mohya has become an increasingly influential figure under Alexander Blessin, with his ability to beat defenders, carry possession through midfield, and create opportunities in tight spaces attracting considerable attention.

Mohya turned in a highly noteworthy performance at a recent Bundesliga covered in person by GGFN. Mohya particularly impressed during Gladbach’s recent victory over VfB Stuttgart, contributing a goal and an assist while repeatedly troubling the opposition defense.

His promotion represents another example of Klopp’s willingness to reward emerging Bundesliga talent. Mohya now has an opportunity to demonstrate that he belongs among the country’s leading attacking prospects.

Perhaps the most encouraging aspect of Klopp’s new selection is the continued inclusion of several players who received their first senior international opportunities during his opening camp. Five recent debutants have retained their places: Bayern Munich goalkeeper Jonas Urbig, Köln attacker Said El Mala, Freiburg duo Philipp Treu and Derry Scherhant, and Eintracht Frankfurt striker Younes Ebnoutalib.

Treu’s inclusion is particularly significant. The Freiburg fullback impressed during his first three appearances under Klopp, quickly demonstrating an understanding of the team’s attacking patterns and establishing himself as a serious candidate for further international involvement.

Urbig also impressed during his first appearances, keeping clean sheets against Serbia and Greece. His continued presence alongside Marc-André ter Stegen and Alexander Nübel ensures that the goalkeeper competition remains an interesting element of Klopp’s squad construction.

El Mala, Scherhant, and Ebnoutalib have likewise earned further opportunities after their initial exposure to senior international football. Two additional players remain in contention for their debuts. VfB Stuttgart defender Finn Jeltsch and RB Leipzig attacker Brajan Gruda were included in the previous selection but have yet to feature for the senior team. Gruda was forced to withdraw through injury.

The reduction from 44 players to 24 inevitably leaves several interesting names outside Klopp’s plans for November. RB Leipzig captain David Raum is one of the most prominent absentees. The 28-year-old has earned 40 senior international caps and previously occupied an important role on Germany’s left defensive flank.

Klopp has instead selected Bayern Munich’s Nathaniel Brown and Stuttgart’s Maximilian Mittelstädt. The omission of Raum suggests that competition for places in Germany’s defensive setup is becoming increasingly intense, although it would be premature to interpret his absence as a permanent demotion.

Woltemade’s absence is particularly noteworthy given Germany’s limited attacking output during the opening Nations League fixtures. The Juventus striker was involved during Klopp’s previous international window but has not retained his place in the reduced squad. Adeyemi’s exclusion likewise removes a player capable of providing pace and directness in wide attacking areas.

Klopp has previously emphasized that players omitted from individual squads should not consider themselves permanently excluded. Nevertheless, the latest selection offers a clearer indication of which candidates currently enjoy his confidence.

Not every prominent absentee has been left out for sporting reasons. Bayern Munich attackers Jamal Musiala and Serge Gnabry are both unavailable through injury, depriving Klopp of two experienced international options.

Musiala’s absence is particularly frustrating given the circumstances of Germany’s previous international window. The attacking midfielder suffered a muscle-fibre tear in his right thigh during the warm-up before September’s 1-1 draw against the Netherlands.

The setback forced Klopp into an unexpected change to his starting lineup and subsequently resulted in Musiala leaving the national-team camp for treatment. Gnabry, meanwhile, suffered a muscular injury to his left hamstring during Bayern’s recent 2-2 Bundesliga draw against Borussia Dortmund.

Their absences reduce Germany’s attacking experience at a time when Klopp is attempting to establish greater consistency in the final third. They also create further opportunities for younger players. Lennart Karl, El Mala, Scherhant, Gruda, and Mohya will all be hoping to strengthen their claims while more established attacking figures remain unavailable.

What Does a Green Porch Light Mean? The Tradition, Explained

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

By Savannah Sher, Updated by Daryna Tobey

Published Nov 5, 2026 12:01 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

In November, you may notice your neighbors replacing their white porch light bulbs with green bulbs. While it’s easy to assume that they’re getting a head start on their Christmas decorating, green porch lights have a significance that has nothing to do with the holiday season. Americans use green porch lights to show support for U.S. military veterans. The idea began as part of the “Greenlight a Vet” project. According to the campaign, “The simple action of changing one light to green is intended to spark a national conversation regarding the recognition of veterans, and ‘greenlight’ them forward as valued members of our communities.”

In 2013, Walmart’s Veterans Welcome Home Commitment initiative, which provides jobs to any honorably discharged U.S. veteran, created an ad campaign asking people to turn on a green porch light as a way to recognize and show appreciation for veterans in their community. They encouraged people to share their support on social media by taking a photo of their green light and sharing it with the hashtag #GreenlightAVet.

The Greenlight a Vet initiative is not the only one to shine a light, so to speak, on our country’s veterans. In 2022, The National Association of Counties (NACo) in affiliation with the National Association of County Veterans Services Officers (NACVSO), launched Operation Green Light for Veterans (#OperationGreenLight). In the program’s debut year, over 300 counties participated nationwide, including Fairbanks, Alaska’s North Star Borough and Mohave County, Arizona. Nicole Weissman, NACo’s director of strategic communications, expects the number of participating counties this year to be “significantly larger” than it was last year.

“We must support our veterans and their families—not only during Operation Green Light but all year round,” says NACo President Jo McGuire. “Counties are advocating for legislation to secure appropriate resources at the local level to help veterans access their federal benefits, ease their transition into civilian life, and support their long-term health and well-being.”

The association explains, “By shining a green light, county governments and our residents will let veterans know that they are seen, appreciated, and supported.” NACo’s initiative encourages all U.S. counties and parishes to show support for military veterans by illuminating courthouses, offices, and other municipal buildings in green from November 4 to 11, the week leading up to Veterans Day.

In September 2023, the House of Representatives introduced House Resolution 750, a bipartisan bill recognizing Operation Green Light for Veterans for “supporting veterans and…help[ing] veterans transition to civilian life.”

Illuminate Enbrighten’s waterproof Eternity lights green for Veterans Day and leave them up year round, changing colors and modes via app.

Green is a color that is often associated with the military because of the green uniforms traditionally worn by troops, but there’s a more symbolic meaning as well. According to the Greenlight a Vet campaign, “Green is the color of hope, renewal and well-being.” They add that “‘Greenlight’ is also a term commonly used to activate forward movement.”

Govee’s smart light bulbs support millions of hues and can be controlled by phone app. Read the review.

While Veterans Day on November 11 is the most symbolic time of year to show support for America’s troops, why not follow Operation Green Light for Veterans’ lead and shine the light for the whole week of Veterans Day, or even keep your green light up year-round to show your ongoing appreciation for our military?

In Prattville, Alabama, community advocate Julie Cooley, who is also a military spouse and mother, went a little further. She encouraged “neighbors, municipalities and businesses to show their love and support of our veterans this week. Some choose to put signage on their marquis, some offer discounts, some turn their lights green! What’s important is that our military veterans and their families feel a great big hug from their Prattville community.”

“One of NACo’s goals for Operation Green Light is to equip counties to participate in a way that’s meaningful to veterans in their community, so we’re always looking for ways to do that,” says Weissman. In some counties, she says, this outreach includes “distributing green light bulbs or otherwise encouraging residents to participate” in Operation Green Light for Veterans.  If you’d like to show your support for veterans this month, pick up a green light bulb at your local hardware store—or at one of the following retailers.

Prices provided here are accurate as of November 5, 2026.

XRP Ledger closes loophole that could let token burners mint new supply

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The XRP Ledger has activated the fixCleanup3_4_0 amendment to close a security loophole in its permission delegation system.

Previously, an account given permission to burn tokens could also create new tokens under certain conditions. The fix blocks this unless the account also has permission to mint tokens.

The amendment also introduced fixes across several network features. These included Single Asset Vaults, lending, automated market makers (AMMs), the permissioned DEX, trust lines, NFTs, Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs), NFTs and escrow. Other fixes covered sponsorship, transaction signing, and permission delegation.

These fixes are mainly designed to address technical issues involving asset calculations, token transfers, liquidity pool operations, account permissions, and reserve requirements. For example, the AMM fix would now prevent liquidity providers from losing their pool tokens due to rounding errors during certain clawback operations.

Similarly, improvements to lending and vault operations would now result in accurate accounting and asset management. The update also boosts MPT transaction validation and resolves issues that could cause NFT offers to fail. All in all, the amendment aims to improve the reliability, security, and functionality of the XRP Ledger’s existing financial infrastructure.

That said, its activation followed two weeks of uninterrupted validator support, as XRPL amendments required more than 80% approval from trusted validators.

This comes as the XRP Ledger ranks ninth among networks in the RWA League Table, with $644.6 million in distributed tokenized assets, representing a 29.20% increase over the past month.

This followed a recent report from AMBCrypto, which indicated that XRPL ranked 10th in tokenized assets, with $642.5 million just a week ago.

Meanwhile, Santiment’s data for XRP Ledger suggests that its Total Open Interest fell from approximately $1.3 billion to below $1 billion towards the end of October. However, it subsequently witnessed a sharp recovery, reaching approximately $1.15 billion at press time.

At the same time, the Stablecoin Supply Ratio stood at 31.31 at the time of writing, marking a decline from around 34.5 to below 30 before recovering.

Inside LA Design Week/end 2026: A City of Creative Connections

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

When New Yorkers land in Los Angeles, the first thing that rattles them is, “Where is everyone?” Unlike the bustling life on Manhattan sidewalks, people in LA tend to stay in their bubbles. Life in this city is deeply insular that way. But you adapt. You curate a bubble of haunts and neighborhoods, and then you navigate to those bubbles from inside another bubble: your car, or, if you’re Gen Z, a Waymo.

Holland Denvir tackled that problem head-on in 2024 when they co-founded LA Design Week/end (LAD/W) alongside Meghan McNeer, who serves as creative director. Now in its third year, the festival brought together more than 130 designers, studios, galleries, and brands across more than 15 Los Angeles neighborhoods during the first week of October, drawing thousands of locals and visitors beyond the beaches and Hollywood sign to discover what makes this city tick: its creative community.

LA Design Week/end co-founder Holland Denvir helped bring together more than 130 designers, studios, galleries, and brands for the festival’s third edition. Photography by Michelle Pullman.

Attempting to make the rounds at all the studios involved would be overwhelming, but like a Choose Your Own Adventure book, the fun is in discovering how many versions of the festival you can experience. What follows is my experience and chosen stops at LA Design Week/end 2026.

Work and gallery spaces inside Small Green Door. Photography by Sydni Stearns.

My first stop was Small Green Door (SGD), a creative studio set in an unassuming cluster of warehouses on the only tree-lined street in Boyle Heights’ industrial zone. A bright soul waved me into the courtyard—Denvir, as I later learned. There, we met SGD’s co-founder Leo Estévez, who graciously walked us through the complex, which includes artist workspaces, an R&D kitchen, several galleries, and even a bunker.

After the Tide, an open-call group show curated by Nadia Khagani and Rose Zhang, gathered 19 artists and designers whose work is shaped by the forces of nature—mostly ceramics, many with a gothic edge. A sonic water installation by Edge Sound Research grounded the presentation, alongside sand pedestals made at the studio. While circulating through the sea of sand pedestals, I couldn’t help imagining the bay scallop and pineapple guava aguachile I’d kill to serve in Kimberly Meyer’s shallow clay bowls, featured in the show.

Before leaving SGD, Estévez handed out retro cassette cases filled with mini zines. Like a secret skate spot of yesteryear, each held a hidden green key, which supposedly gets you into the studio.

Kimberly Meyer’s Shallow Clay Bowls from ‘After the Tide,’ an open-call group show curated by Nadia Khagani and Rose Zhang at Small Green Door in Los Angeles. Photography by TJ Girard.

Over lunch with Holland at Henrietta in Echo Park, we chatted about the festival’s beginnings and evolution. They chose this small, unassuming brick storefront—a market-driven sandwich shop by day and California bistro by night, helmed by owner Mark Lesser. He regaled us with stories of growing up in the storage room of his mother’s toy store back east and the arduous process of ordering the understated tiles lining the counter’s façade.

Henrietta is a quintessential neighborhood spot with a gourmet bodega-like corner for picking up giftable olive oil, fancy chocolate bars, and bottles of wine. But the mountainous, snowy Parmesan-capped radicchio salad is what stays with me, along with the most decadent fried smashed potatoes, which were impossible to leave unfinished.

Between courses, Holland explained the thinking behind LAD/W’s approach: “We want LAD/W to be accessible to everyone. Whether you’re in the design industry or not. We want people to come discover and connect with the creatives that make LA special.”

The format is deliberately decentralized. Rather than being anchored to a convention center or a single design district, LAD/W unfolds across the city: each day centers on one or a cluster of Eastside neighborhoods, with events planned around one another so you can move by foot, bike, scooter, or Metro.

“Leave your car at home? In LA?” I asked Holland.

“I want every moment you spend participating to be about discovery and connection, not sitting in your car in traffic; we can do that any other day of the week,” they replied.

To prove this point, after lunch we walked down the block to see Our Joy is Resistance at Alabaster Gallery, hosted by Enzo. But what came next was a true example of why I really do love Los Angeles.

‘Our Joy is Resistance’ at Alabaster Gallery. Photography by Sydni Stearns.

Enter the Living Paul McCobb Museum, where I met Yogi Proctor and, later that evening, his wife, documentary filmmaker Carol Martori. Proctor, a British transplant who came to the States through skateboarding, turned his private obsession into a living museum dedicated to collecting, preserving, and studying McCobb’s 1950s Planner Group furniture and its accompanying accessory lines.

Vignettes from inside the Living Paul McCobb Museum. Photography by TJ Girard.

In the 1950s, Paul McCobb’s name was synonymous with American-made furniture. The Manhattan-based designer was even among the first to embrace the idea of a designer’s signature as a brand. By adding a watermark stamp at first, and later a foil mark, McCobb made his work easy to identify.

A collection must start somewhere: I learned Proctor first came across a McCobb piece while living in Chicago. After 20 years of collecting, his midcentury Silver Lake home (built in 1949 and remodeled in 2017) now houses a third of his collection.

Vignettes from inside the Living Paul McCobb Museum. Photography by TJ Girard.

Walking through the museum, you are surrounded by McCobb, stacked on top of more McCobb. At one point, for fun, I started to look for the non-McCobb items: a cat sculpture by the front entrance, a wall of books in the study, and anniversary cards on the nightstand. But back in the living room, the bright colors and remarkably minimal shapes of the Contempri tableware and functional objects on display caught my eye.

Proctor explained that in 1957, McCobb worked with porcelain makers in Japan on what became an early example of dishwasher-safe porcelain tableware. We talked about the trickiness of a living museum—the wear and tear that comes with actually using the objects day to day. I confessed my own hesitation about using my Wagenfeld-designed Kubus glass storage jars for fear of chipping one. But like McCobb’s work, the design is simply too brilliant not to use.

Vibrant and colorful Contempri tableware on display inside the Paul McCobb Living Museum. Photography by TJ Girard.

I then made my way to the Silver Lake Reservoir and Richard Neutra’s former home, now the Neutra VDL Studio and Residences, a National Historic Landmark and house museum committed to education, architecture, art, and culture. There, the Yamaha Design Laboratory, the company’s in-house design division, established in 1963, presented Companionship. The exhibit positioned musical instruments as companions that stay by one’s side throughout life. Ten design works and instruments were placed throughout the space for visitors to explore.

Classic guitar stand-stool designed for living with the instrument. Photography courtesy of Yamaha.

On the ground floor, we met multimedia artist and photographer Johnny Le, who served as guest curator. His photographs, taken at Yamaha’s headquarters and piano factory in Japan, were also on view. The exploded overhead view of every single part of a Yamaha piano was quite impressive. Then we moved upstairs to experience two guitar stand-stools designed for living with the instrument, and the TransAcoustic guitar, which can play music via Bluetooth.

The exhibition also included the first public showing of a prototype for Dreaming of the Sea, a low chair made with Karimoku Case that produces soothing, wave-like sounds when you sit on the cushion and move gently. The small metal balls inside roll and rub against one another, shifting from crashing waves to quiet ripples.

We ended our visit on the roof, where Manabu Kawada’s The Backrest for Sky Viewers was on display. According to Yamaha Design Laboratory, while leaning back on the plank of wood and looking up at the endless bright sky, everybody can believe in the beauty of the world.

Prototype for “Dreaming of the Sea,” a low chair made with Karimoku Case and Yamaha Design Lab. Photography courtesy of Yamaha.

By evening, I made my way to the Haas Brothers‘ studio, ready to party. Once again, the building’s exterior was unassuming—but once inside, you couldn’t help but smile with delight, surrounded by whimsical sculptures scattered throughout the fabrication shop where they were made.

The food displays by Zehra Ahmed were fittingly sculptural and playful. Among a dotted line of long-stemmed roses, two serpentine sandwiches made with braided brioche stretched more than 10 feet long atop a suede-like tablecloth, split apart and laced together to form a runner. I spent an embarrassing amount of time trying to find the seams that achieved this grand length, and I was admittedly very impressed with the execution. Dessert was a rectangular bed of sugar-dusted doughnut holes with three dipping sauces on pedestals. It felt a bit underwhelming by comparison. It wasn’t until the end of the evening that I stumbled onto the back patio, where freshly made wood-fired pizzas and a selection of natural wines were being served.

A food display by Zehra Ahmed at Haas Brothers LAD/W opening event. Photography by TJ Girard

One thing I’m still sorry to have missed: Los Angeles Sets a Table at Ravenhill Studio. The space featured two tables representing dawn and dusk, with contrasting palettes that brought clarity to a wide range of work. Cathy Callahan initially had the idea back in 2019, and it finally came together this year with curators Chelito Villaflor and Chris Albanese of Thought Forms Studio. The tablescape exhibition celebrated gathering in real life around things made by hand.

‘Los Angeles Sets a Table’ at Ravenhill Studio. Photography by Christopher Dibble.

If this edition of LAD/W proved anything, it’s that LA’s design community is bringing people together without a convention center, inviting them to leave their bubbles and stroll through someone else’s. Though many people I met were from LA or had lived here for decades, the recurring thread throughout my conversations was the pleasure of experiencing these particular parts of the city through studio visits. And of course, the people-watching and outfits at the evening events almost superseded the artwork and design on display!

TJ Girard is a food designer and creative consultant known for creating theatrical, interactive food and beverage experiences. Based in California, she brings together culinary arts, design, and storytelling to create memorable, multi-sensory events.

You can follow TJ Girard on Instagram, and Linkedin. Read all of TJ Girard's posts.

From experimental ceramics and midcentury furniture to interactive instruments and sculptural food, LA Design Week/end invites visitors to explore the city's creative community, one neighborhood at a time

A visit to SingleThread Farm for its annual FieldNotes gathering reveals the art of donabe cooking, a centuries-old Japanese tradition

'The Curious Lives of Vegetables' looks beyond the plate to uncover the history, biodiversity, and beauty hiding in the foods we know best

With BUILDING BLOCK ETC., sisters Kimberly and Nancy Wu recalibrate their design language through entertaining objects, curated materials, and a retail experience rooted in touch.
