How Kimi Antonelli Trains His Brain for Peak Speed—and an F1 Record

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

It’s official, 20-year-old Kimi Antonelli has secured his spot as the youngest F1 Champion in history after just two seasons, but while the dedicated Mercedes driver has been relentless on the track, the speedy Italian has also stayed active outside of his car. Here’s why the history maker’s appetite for adventure is the reason that he never stays still, and how a smart approach to staying active benefits both body and mind.

Kimi Antonelli’s third place in Mexico City on November 1 meant that he’d earned enough points to stave off all other drivers to complete the season as champion, but this overnight success is the result of a mature approach to staying sharp. “I try to do things that keep me active, but won’t tire me out,” explained Antonelli during a recent and telling interview with GQ. “It’s all a way to release mental stress, to just relax and enjoy. I try to do these activities as much as possible because in sports, the mental [part] is basically what does everything.”

The activities that Antonelli spoke of include race related training such as simulators, go-karts, and operating remote controlled cars, but he’s also a keen proponent of padel. Not only has the young upstart played against fellow Mercedes drivers like George Russell, but he’s even been known to stroke the ball from the deck of a super yacht from time to time.

Antonelli’s fuel for dominating F1 was on show early, as he transitioned from Formula 2 and continued his approach to “hard” training, sharing that a day of working out included “An early morning swim, neck training at lunch, and an afternoon bike ride.”

The inspirational Italian is also hot with the hoops, and in a recent Instagram Reel revealed that he can make a basket from an impressive distance, all while standing on one leg!

While Antonelli’s love of fun activities helps him keep a positive mindset as he works towards pole position, science has also shown that becoming skilled in a variety of sporting activities can only help with his day job. Activities such as shooting baskets, stroking the Padel ball, and other loves such as surfing, no doubt boost his reaction times. In fact, a scientific study in 2023 confirmed that reaction training (they used flashing lights as the stimuli) induced beneficial effects on physiological and cognitive performance.

At just 20 years of age, Kimi Antonelli has the world at his highly successful feet. So, what’s next? “Right now, my main goal is to give everything to F1, but in my normal life, I want to be successful, too,” he revealed to GQ. “I want to be efficient, and in 20 to 30 years’ time, when I won’t be racing anymore, I want to still do something that keeps me active. Because once you finish, it’s easy to do nothing after working hard for so many years. I want to be successful outside of racing.”

With an attitude like Antonelli’s, the journey is far from over.

meet the wienermobile, the 1930s quirky pedal car that put hot dogs on wheels

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

from its first appearance on american roads in 1936 to its miniature interpretations by bird corporation and konek, the oversized hot dog has become an enduring piece of advertising and automotive culture.

First introduced in 1936, the Oscar Mayer Wienermobile began as a traveling advertisement shaped like an oversized hot dog. Over the decades, the vehicle has gone through several redesigns, but its distinctive silhouette has remained central to the identity of the brand, turning an unconventional marketing idea into a familiar sight on American roads. Its recognizable form has also inspired toys, merchandise, and unusual experiences. In 2019, Oscar Mayer even transformed a full-size Wienermobile into a one-night Airbnb stay in Chicago, inviting guests to eat, live, and sleep inside the eight-meter-long vehicle (find designboom’s previous coverage here).

Now, two vintage Oscar Mayer Wienermobile pedal cars, produced by Bird Corporation and Konek Inc., are appearing together at auction on Bring a Trailer. Offered as a single lot without a reserve, the two new-old-stock examples are described as having never been ridden by children.

The story of the Wienermobile begins in 1936, when Carl G. Mayer, nephew of Oscar Mayer founder Oscar F. Mayer, proposed a hot dog-shaped vehicle as a traveling advertisement for the company’s products. Built by Chicago’s General Body Company, the original measured approximately four meters long and featured a metal body shaped like an oversized wiener, mounted on wheels. Its two open cockpits accommodated a driver and Little Oscar, the promotional character of the brand.

As the vehicle became an established part of Oscar Mayer’s advertising, successive generations introduced new interpretations of the original concept. The 1958 model, designed by industrial designer Brooks Stevens, introduced a rounded, bubble-like front and more integrated bun-shaped elements. Later versions experimented with larger bodies, updated chassis, and elaborate interiors, always retaining the unmistakable hot dog silhouette.

Oscar Mayer discontinued the program in the late 1970s, shifting its attention toward television advertising. Public enthusiasm for the vehicle, however, prompted its return in 1986 for the company’s 50th anniversary. New generations followed, and the Wienermobile resumed its travels across the United States, appearing at parades, public gatherings, supermarkets, and promotional events.

Over the years, the hot dog on wheels inspired toys, merchandise, and other branded interpretations, translating its instantly recognizable shape into objects that could be collected and played with. The two pedal cars now offered at auction on Bring a Trailer belong to this broader history.

Although they share the same orange-and-yellow color scheme, the Bird Corporation and Konek examples feature different construction methods. The Bird version has a single-piece, hot-dog-shaped plastic body mounted over a yellow base, while the Konek model uses a two-piece orange body with Oscar Mayer and Wienermobile decals across its front, rear, and sides.

Both combine molded plastic exteriors with metal frames, orange wheels, yellow seats, and black steering wheels with yellow horn buttons. Twin foot pedals power the miniature vehicles, while faux headlights, grilles, taillights, and windows complete their automotive appearance. The Konek example measures approximately 114 centimeters long, 53 centimeters wide, and 48 centimeters tall.

Today, the Wienermobile remains part of Oscar Mayer’s identity, with six vehicles operated by parent company Kraft Heinz. Its continued presence on American roads speaks to the unusual longevity of a marketing idea conceived in the 1930s. The hot dog-shaped vehicle has become a recognizable piece of American visual culture and continues to inspire new interpretations nearly 90 years after its first appearance.

The two pedal cars’ appearance at auction offers a chance to revisit the history of the Wienermobile, a promotional vehicle that brought advertising, automotive design, and popular culture together. First introduced in 1936, the hot dog-shaped vehicle has evolved into an enduring fixture of American popular culture.

Sony is enjoying a sharp profit spike in its latest quarterly financials, largely driven by its PlayStation business and the blockbuster success of Spider-Man: Brand New Day. According to the company’s September earnings, Sony’s net profits climbed to 449.60 billion yen (about $2.84 billion) for the three months ended September — a 44 percent increase compared to the 311.4 billion yen ($1.96 billion) it reported for the same period last year.

Spider-Man: Brand New Day was a notable contributor, which released in July and became the third-highest grossing movie in history by earning $2.52 billion at the global box office. That’s a huge boost for Sony Pictures Entertainment, which posted earnings of $581 million for the three months ended September — a 527 percent jump from the $93 million it reported for the same period in 2025.

Sales of Playstation 5 consoles have fallen, with 2.6 million units reported this second quarter compared to the 4 million that were sold in Q2 2025. While the Sony-exclusive Marvel’s Wolverine sold 2.4 million units against a reported budget of $300 million, PlayStation game sales generally have increased for both digital downloads and physical discs. That’s in spite of the week-long player blackout that took place in August to protest Sony’s plan to ditch discs entirely. Looking forward, PlayStation is now looking at its next generation console, which developers are making “solid progress” on according to Sony CFO Lin Tao, and is “progressing as scheduled.”

Consumers are spending on physical gifts even as travel tops wish lists, Visa survey finds

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Consumers are hoping for more travel and experiences this holiday season, but physical gifts continue to win the lion's share of their wallets, according to a new report from Visa on Thursday.

The report found that more than half of survey respondents are putting travel and live events at the top of their wish lists. Still, only 15% of those respondents are prioritizing spending on experiences for others, while 27% of them plan to favor physical gifts.

That gap between what shoppers want and what they plan to buy is largely due to higher travel costs, lower consumer sentiment and an emphasis on value, according to Michael Brown, Visa's principal U.S. economist.

"There is some interesting trade-offs there that we're seeing on the part of consumers," Brown told CNBC. "If I'm traveling more, I'm probably going to be gifting those physical goods less, so this is really the broader context."

The survey was conducted among roughly 4,660 U.S. adults ages 18 and older, designed to be representative of the broader population of consumers.

Brown said budget pressure also factors into traveling for the holidays, as Visa expects more people to celebrate the holidays "a little bit closer to home" than in the past given rising airfares and general inflation. In September, consumer prices rose 3.7% annually, driven higher by surging energy costs.

Airlines have hiked the price of plane tickets this year as their fuel costs have risen by billions of dollars due to the war in the Middle East. And even if fuel prices drop, executives have said fares are likely to stay high, with fares up more than 22% in September compared with a year earlier, according to the consumer price index.

Those trends mean consumers will likely be more intentional about where they're spending, especially as inflation drives up prices across the board.

"Consumers are aspirational. They want those experiences, they want to travel, they want to be out in the world," Brown said. "However, the economic reality is with prices rising, with economic uncertainty, they need to balance the reality of today's economic environment with those aspirations."

The report also found that only households with an income above $200,000 spent more on trips departing between Nov. 1 and early next year compared with 2025 into 2026. On the other hand, many other income groups pulled back on their travel spending.

"If you think about that segment, a lot of them own equities, and the equity market runup over the last several months has created a lot of positivity among that specific affluent demographic, and I think that's one of the factors that continues to motivate even aggregate macro spend today," Brown said.

Meanwhile, less affluent shoppers are placing an emphasis on value. Visa's consumer survey from October showed that roughly one-third of consumers with an income below $100,000 are going to switch to lower-cost brands or stores when shopping for holiday gifts this season.

Though Visa is projecting a 4.5% year-over-year increase in holiday sales growth, much of that figure is due to inflation. Without those price increases, sales are expected to rise just 1.1% this year, down from the 1.3% increase during the 2025 holiday season.

Ultimately, Brown said the survey findings may be encouraging for the broader retail scene this holiday season. With less emphasis on travel and more projected spending on physical gifts, it could boost the sector at a time when spending is under pressure.

At the same time, he said that consumers may just be expecting to spend more this holiday season because they've become accustomed to the higher prices.

"But, of course, consumers are internalizing higher prices of things, and I think that's why they're reporting higher anticipated spend this year," Brown said. "I think these numbers just need to be looked at through the lens of the inflation and particularly consumers' perception of where inflation is going to go. I think that weighed heavily on consumer psychology this season."

Spinach Artichoke Baked Potatoes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This baked potato stuffed with spinach-artichoke dip is the ultimate solo comfort meal. Classic twice-baked potatoes require two trips to the oven to deliver a pillowy, gussied-up filling and crispy skins all in one bite. Here, I’ve streamlined the workflow with a microwave. By zapping your spuds to start, you can reduce the overall baking time without sacrificing texture. Pick a large russet (or Idaho) for the best results; their relatively high starch and low moisture content helps them bake up airy and light, not gummy.

The rich, velvety filling that melts over top comes together in moments while your spud cooks. Cream cheese and mozzarella pack in enough dairy richness for a satisfying cheese pull and mellow out the subtle bitterness of baby spinach and nutty tang of artichoke hearts. (It’s important that your cream cheese is room temperature before you mash it in with the veggies. If it’s still got a chill from the fridge, it won’t fully incorporate.) The Worcestershire sauce is optional, but just a few shakes from the bottle bring instant savory depth to the filling.

Most recipes serve multiple people. In Me, Myself, and Dinner, each dish serves one and only one. That's you! Because whether you live solo (almost 30% of Americans do) or are enjoying a night to yourself, sitting down to a meal should be something to look forward to.

Preheat oven to 425°. Poke 1 large russet potato (about 12 oz.) all over with a fork; rub with avocado or vegetable oil to coat and sprinkle with kosher salt. Microwave potato on a microwave-proof plate on high 5 minutes. Turn potato over and microwave on high until fork-tender, about 5 minutes more. Let potato sit until just cool enough to handle.

While potato is cooling, microwave 5 oz. baby spinach with 2 Tbsp. water in a medium microwave-proof glass bowl until wilted, about 2 minutes. Carefully press leaves to release as much liquid as possible, then drain off liquid. Coarsely chop spinach.

Mix 2 Tbsp. cream cheese, room temperature, in a medium bowl until smooth. Add spinach, 4 quartered artichoke hearts (from a 14-oz. can), coarsely chopped, 2 Tbsp. finely grated Parmesan, 2 Tbsp. coarsely grated mozzarella, ½ tsp. garlic powder, and ¼ tsp. Worcestershire sauce (if using) and fold to combine; season with salt and freshly ground pepper.

Slice potato in half lengthwise and lightly fluff flesh in each half. Set on a small foil-lined rimmed baking sheet. Divide spinach mixture between potato halves, piling high (don’t pack too tightly). Sprinkle more Parmesan over. Bake potato halves until very tender when squeezed and skin is wrinkled and crisp, 20–25 minutes. Let cool slightly before eating.

Business class hops across Europe for fewer points than most domestic economy awards

Picture this: you've just crossed the Atlantic on a long-haul award, touched down at London Heathrow, and now you need to get to Rome for the real adventure to begin. Most travelers at this point resign themselves to a cramped economy seat on a budget carrier, dragging luggage through a secondary terminal. But you? You're walking into the Virgin Atlantic clubhouse, sipping a Bloody Mary, and then settling into a proper business class seat for the 2.5-hour hop to Fiumicino. That's the magic of this redemption. Here's the math that should make your jaw drop. A business class seat on a short-haul European flight — London Heathrow to Rome, London to Athens, London to virtually any major European city — routinely retails for **$1,000 to $3,000 in cash**. Airlines know that business travelers on these routes are often on corporate accounts and will pay whatever it costs. The leisure traveler simply doesn't book business class on a two-hour flight because it feels absurd. And that's exactly the pricing inefficiency the ChooseFI community learns to exploit. Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club prices these short-haul European business class seats at a flat **17,500 miles one-way**. At the low end of that $1,000–$3,000 cash range, you're already extracting **5.7 cents per point** — well above what most programs deliver. At the high end, when those seats spike to $3,000 on peak travel dates, you're looking at a staggering **17.1 cents per point**. For context, most travel rewards experts consider 1.5–2 cents per point a solid redemption. This one can deliver *ten times* that. What makes this feel particularly right for the FI mindset is the positioning angle. This isn't just a standalone splurge — it's the finishing move on a transatlantic trip you've already largely funded with points. You've landed in London. You need to get to your final European destination. Instead of paying $200 for a budget airline seat that charges extra for your carry-on, you drop 17,500 Virgin miles and arrive refreshed, fed, and ready. The opportunity cost of those miles is low; the experiential upgrade is enormous. This is what travel rewards done right looks like: not flying business class because you're wealthy, but because you understood the system better than the airlines expected you to.

The core insight here is understanding how Virgin Atlantic prices its own metal versus partner awards — and then deliberately choosing the right one for the right job.

Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club is famous in the points community for two things. First, its partner award sweet spots: ANA First Class for as low as 55,000–60,000 miles one-way, Delta One at competitive rates. These are the headline acts. But quietly sitting in the same award chart is a genuinely underappreciated gem — Virgin's own short-haul European business class, priced at a flat 17,500 miles regardless of the specific route within that distance band.

Why does Virgin price it this way? Virgin Atlantic operates as a premium carrier and sets its award chart to reflect aspirational redemptions — the kind that make Flying Club membership feel worthwhile. For long-haul routes on their own metal, their pricing is competitive but not exceptional. For short-haul Europe, however, the fixed pricing creates an opportunity: a two-hour flight to Rome or Athens costs the same 17,500 miles as a cheaper, shorter hop, but those premium routes have cash prices that routinely spike into the thousands. The chart wasn't built to be a loophole — it's just that cash prices for short-haul business class in Europe are genuinely volatile and often extraordinary.

The fuel surcharge reality check. Here's where you need to pay close attention. Virgin Atlantic is known for passing fuel surcharges onto award redemptions on their own flights. This is the opposite of what happens on ANA or Delta partner awards booked through Flying Club (which carry zero surcharges). On a short-haul European route, those surcharges are real but typically far more manageable than on a transatlantic segment — often in the $50–$150 range rather than $500+. The CPP math still works strongly in your favor, but you should go in with eyes open: this is not a zero-cash-outlay redemption.

The transfer path is exceptional. This is where the ChooseFI community has a serious structural advantage. Virgin Atlantic Flying Club accepts transfers from essentially every major points currency:

Every major transferable currency gets you there at a 1:1 ratio with instant speed. That means if you're holding Chase points from your Sapphire Reserve or Preferred, Amex points from your Gold or Platinum, or Citi points from your Premier card, you have a direct, frictionless pipeline to 17,500 Virgin miles within minutes of finding availability. This is critical because, as you'll learn in the booking section, Virgin availability on its own short-haul European routes can be limited, and you want to be able to move fast when a seat opens up.

The booking window opens 331 days in advance — use it. Miles don't expire as long as you have account activity within 36 months, so there's no pressure to burn them before you're ready.

Before you touch a single website, clarify your mission: you're looking for Virgin Atlantic-operated business class availability on short-haul European routes departing from London Heathrow (LHR). This is not a search for partner award space — this is VS metal, VS availability, VS seats. Your most common target routes will be LHR → FCO (Rome), LHR → ATH (Athens), and similar European city pairs. Build your itinerary around where VS actually flies in Europe, not every possible destination.

Go to virginatlantic.com and use the award search tool. Select "Book with miles" and enter your route. Virgin shows its own availability directly here — you don't need a third-party search engine for this specific redemption. Search one-way rather than round-trip so you can see each direction independently. If your target dates show no availability, flex by ±3 days. Business class award seats on short-haul routes are typically released in small batches.

Open a second browser tab and search the same route and date on Google Flights or directly on virginatlantic.com for a revenue ticket. Note the cash price for business class. This is your CPP sanity check — if the cash price is $1,200 and you're spending 17,500 miles plus ~$100 in surcharges, you're at roughly 6.3 cents per point. If the cash price is $2,500, your CPP jumps dramatically. This step helps you prioritize which dates to target.

Log into your Virgin Atlantic Flying Club account. If you don't have 17,500 miles already, check your transferable points balances:

Do NOT transfer points until you have confirmed award availability on your target date. Transfers to Virgin are one-directional and cannot be reversed.

Once you've confirmed a seat is available, transfer your points from your chosen program. For Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt transfers, the miles land in your Flying Club account instantly. Refresh your Flying Club account, confirm the miles are there, and complete the booking before the seat disappears. Enter your payment details for the taxes and surcharges (typically $50–$150 on short-haul European routes).

If you're struggling to find seats close to your travel dates, shift your strategy to the booking window's leading edge. Virgin opens award availability 331 days in advance. Set a calendar reminder 331 days before your ideal travel date, log in at that exact time, and book the moment inventory releases. Peak-season seats to popular destinations like Rome and Athens move quickly.

After booking, screenshot your confirmation, note your booking reference, and verify the routing, cabin class, and surcharge amounts. Add the flight to your calendar with the Virgin Atlantic booking reference. Sign up for flight notifications.

Virgin Atlantic operates its own short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations across Europe including Amsterdam, Paris, Edinburgh, Geneva, and Athens. These are different from partner codeshare flights. The 17,500-point price applies specifically to Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class. Partner flights (booked through Flying Club but operated by Delta, KLM, or Air France) use a different award chart and typically cost significantly more. When searching, filter for Virgin Atlantic flight numbers (VS prefix) to find the sweet spot pricing. The business class product on short-haul includes a dedicated cabin, lounge access at Heathrow, priority boarding, and meal service.

Let's walk through a real scenario that illustrates exactly what this redemption looks like in practice.

The Setup: You've just arrived at London Heathrow on a transatlantic award — perhaps an ANA First Class redemption through the very same Flying Club program. You have a few days in London, and now you need to get to Athens, Greece, where your Mediterranean adventure begins. You check flights: a business class seat on Virgin Atlantic's LHR → ATH route on your target date is priced at $2,200 cash.

The Redemption: You log into virginatlantic.com and find a business class award seat on that same flight: 17,500 Virgin miles + approximately $120 in taxes and fuel surcharges. You have 20,000 Citi ThankYou Points sitting in your ThankYou account from your Citi Premier card. You initiate the transfer — 1:1, instant — and within minutes, your Flying Club account reflects 17,500 miles (with 2,500 to spare for future use). You complete the booking.

The Experience: On the day of travel, you access the Virgin Clubhouse at Heathrow — one of the most celebrated airport lounges in the world. The cocktail bar, the à la carte dining, the spa treatments available before departure — this is the part that makes the ChooseFI community members shake their heads in disbelief when they first experience it. Then you board and settle into Virgin's short-haul business class: a proper dedicated seat, a full meal service, attentive cabin crew. For a two-and-a-half-hour flight, it's a genuinely elevated experience.

You land in Athens refreshed, fed, and without having spent thousands of dollars. That's the positioning flight done right — and it's what separates travelers who understand the travel rewards system from those who are still paying cash for European business class.

Avoid this: Transferring points before confirming availability This is the single most common mistake. Transferring miles to Virgin Atlantic is a one-way street. If you send 17,500 Chase points over and then discover the seat isn't actually available (or you misread the cabin class), those miles are stuck in Flying Club. Avoid this: Confusing this with partner award bookings Virgin's zero-surcharge policy applies to partner airlines like ANA and Delta. They don't destroy the CPP math, but they're a genuine cash cost you need to plan around.

Do this: Hold Virgin miles for multiple redemptions Because miles don't expire with account activity every 36 months, there's no reason to hoard and rush. Keep Flying Club active by earning miles through their shopping portal or partners, and wait for the right route and cash price moment to pounce.

This redemption is built for a specific type of ChooseFI traveler: the intermediate-to-advanced points strategist who thinks in itineraries, not individual flights.

The ideal candidate is someone who's already planning a trip that routes through London Heathrow — perhaps on a transatlantic award — and needs an elegant, high-value way to reach their final European destination. Solo travelers and couples benefit most here. At 17,500 miles per person, a couple needs 35,000 Virgin miles, which is completely achievable with a single transfer from a combined Chase or Amex balance.

Families should approach with caution. This redemption scores a 2/5 for family-friendliness for good reason. Multiplying 17,500 miles per person across four or more travelers creates a significant points requirement, and availability for multiple seats on the same flight is far from guaranteed. The fuel surcharges also multiply with passenger count. Families are better served by other redemption strategies.

This is NOT for beginners. The overall score of 2.7/5 and beginner-friendliness rating of 1/5 signal real complexity. If you're just starting your travel rewards journey, build your foundation with simpler redemptions first. But if you've got a year of points accumulation behind you and you're ready to think architecturally about trip planning, this sweet spot can be a revelatory experience.

This redemption is rated **Hard** — and that rating is earned honestly. The difficulty isn't in the math or the concept; it's in three very real execution challenges. **Challenge 1: Finding award availability.** Virgin Atlantic doesn't release enormous quantities of business class award seats on its own short-haul European flights. You may search a dozen dates before finding a seat. This is the primary friction point. The fix: use the 331-day advance booking window religiously, and be genuinely flexible on your travel dates by at least ±3 days. **Challenge 2: Navigating the Virgin Atlantic booking interface.** Compared to programs like Chase's travel portal, Flying Club's award search tool is clunkier and less intuitive. It occasionally shows availability that isn't actually bookable through to completion. The fix: search virginatlantic.com directly, be patient with the interface, and have your payment information ready to move quickly when a seat surfaces. **Challenge 3: Understanding the surcharge reality.** New points enthusiasts sometimes expect zero out-of-pocket on award bookings. Virgin's fuel surcharges on its own metal are real, and they'll surprise you if you're not expecting them. The fix: budget $50–$150 per person in cash costs as a baseline assumption. The good news: the **transfer path is as easy as it gets** in the points world. Instant 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt mean that once you find availability, you can have miles in your account and a booking confirmed in under 10 minutes. If you're willing to invest the search time and approach with patience, this is one of those redemptions that fundamentally changes how you see the travel rewards system. The payoff — business class in Europe for 17,500 miles — is worth the effort.

This redemption works best as part of a larger trip. If you are flying transatlantic to or from Europe, route through London Heathrow. Then use the short-haul award to connect to your final European destination at a fraction of the cost of a separate ticket.

Log into VirginAtlantic.com and search for reward flights in Upper Class (business) on your desired European route. Look for VS-coded flights only. Ignore partner-operated results, which will price differently.

Midweek flights (Tuesday through Thursday) and shoulder season dates offer the best availability. Peak summer weekends and holiday periods are harder to find. Build flexibility into your schedule if possible.

Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Flying Club are instant, making Chase the preferred transfer partner for time-sensitive bookings. Amex Membership Rewards also transfers 1:1 but may take 1-2 days.

Complete the booking on VirginAtlantic.com. Your business class ticket includes access to the Virgin Atlantic Clubhouse at Heathrow, one of the best airport lounges in Europe. Arrive early and enjoy it as part of the experience.

Virgin Atlantic operates short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations including Amsterdam, Paris CDG, Edinburgh, Geneva, Athens, Tel Aviv, and several others. Routes change seasonally, so check VirginAtlantic.com for current schedules. The 17,500-point sweet spot applies to all Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class flights.

Yes, Virgin Atlantic Flying Club prices awards as one-way, so you can book a single short-haul business class segment for 17,500 points. This makes it ideal for positioning flights where you only need one direction covered by points.

The flight itself is short, but the value extends beyond the seat. You get dedicated check-in, priority boarding, the Heathrow Clubhouse lounge (which is excellent), a meal service, and a more relaxed travel experience. At 17,500 points, the per-point value (3-5 cents) makes it a strong redemption even for a short flight.

Virgin Atlantic does add taxes and fees to award tickets, including some carrier-imposed surcharges. On short-haul European routes, total out-of-pocket costs typically run $50-$150 depending on the route and departure airport taxes. This is higher than some programs but still reasonable given the business class product.

The Virgin Atlantic short-haul European business class sweet spot is the perfect complement to a transatlantic award trip. At just 17,500 points, it costs less than most domestic economy awards while delivering business class service, Heathrow Clubhouse lounge access, and a stress-free connection to destinations across Europe. It is not the flashiest redemption in travel rewards, but it is one of the most practical: a building block that makes multi-city European itineraries accessible and affordable for the FI community.

New Food Pyramid Puts Healthy Carbs and Plant-Based Foods First

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A first-of-its-kind international food pyramid, developed by 22 leading nutritionists from Europe, Canada, the United States, and Australia, was published in Advances in Nutrition in September.

This new food pyramid emphasizes plant-based protein sources, cooking with plant-based oils, and healthier sugars and carbohydrates.

It also echoes the traditional food pyramid used in the United States since 1992.

The new pyramid breaks away from the Trump administration’s updated nutritional guidelines, which are part of Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s Make America Health Again (MAHA) agenda and prioritize red meat over plants.

The new pyramid was designed by a research team led by Brigham Young University professor Karen Della Corte. The researchers noted that this is the first international food pyramid that is designed to reflect scientific agreement across multiple countries and cultures.

Della Corte and researchers from the International Carbohydrate Quality Consortium (ICQC), compared dietary guidelines from multiple countries, including:

They also analyzed evidence from more than 250 scientific publications, while drawing on the EAT-Lancet Planetary Health Diet to identify principles of healthy eating that may be shared.

The new pyramid highlights three guidelines:

While the pyramid emphasizes plant-based proteins, it doesn’t exclude animal proteins.

“I find the new international food pyramid a more practical and aligned approach for the prevention of chronic disease,” said Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the creation of the food pyramid.

“The new international food pyramid places greater emphasis on food quality and the importance of building meals around nutrient-dense plant foods, while still allowing room for lean animal proteins such as fish, poultry, eggs, and yogurt,” she added.

While the new pyramid and the current U.S. dietary guidelines differ in many ways, they also agree on certain factors.

For example, both sets of guidelines discourage added sugars, refined carbs, and highly processed snacks.

The differences between the two mainly lie in their emphasis on specific sources of protein and fat. For example, the new pyramid prioritizes plant-based proteins, while the U.S. guidelines place greater emphasis on animal proteins, including red meat.

While both sets of recommendations advise limiting saturated fat, the new pyramid emphasizes unsaturated plant oils, whereas the U.S. guidelines also include butter and beef tallow as cooking fat options.

Routhenstein noted that an important distinction is that this new pyramid goes beyond simply telling people to eat more protein or fewer carbohydrates. Instead, it encourages people to consider the quality and sources of those nutrients.

However, the main emphasis of both guidelines is encouraging people to eat fewer ultra-processed foods and more natural or “real” foods.

Routhenstein told Healthline that research consistently shows that plant-forward eating patterns, including the Mediterranean and DASH diets, are associated with better cardiovascular health and a lower risk of heart disease.

Plant foods provide fiber, potassium, antioxidants, and other beneficial compounds that can help support healthy cholesterol levels, blood pressure, blood sugar, and blood vessel function.

“This is not to say that protein and fat are unimportant, but their sources matter,” Routhenstein said.

In a statement from BYU, Della Corte shared the following tips for incorporating the new pyramid’s recommendations into your diet:

The pyramid is a framework, not a personalized prescription. If you have questions about your individual nutritional needs, you can speak with your healthcare professional or a licensed dietitian.

“It is important to recognize that plant-forward does not mean vegan or vegetarian. The amount and sources of animal protein should be considered to ensure they align with an individual’s cardiometabolic goals and lab targets,” Routhenstein said.

This means it is unlikely he plays against Hull. Arsenal are not expected to bring him back too quickly ahead of the international break.

Arteta will miss one of his most influential attackers. Hopefully the next scan will give a clearer indication of how long he is likely to be out for.

Expected return date: Unknown. Saka is not expected to face Hull City on November 7.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-final-2027-outpatient-pay-rule-brings-hospitals-little-relief-340b-site-neutral" hreflang="en">CMS’ final 2027 outpatient pay rule brings hospitals little relief on 340B, site-neutral changes</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →
CMS’ final 2027 outpatient pay rule brings hospitals little relief on 340B, site-neutral changes" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services on Tuesday finalized its annual outpatient hospital payments rule with a lower-than-proposed rate bump and its major, industry-opposed policy shifts moderated, but largely intact.

The 2027 Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment (OPPS) and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) Payment Systems rule outlines a 2.3% reimbursement increase for those meeting applicable quality reporting requirements. That’s below the proposed 2.4% increase and reflects a 3.2% projected hospital market basket increase and a 0.9 percentage point productivity adjustment.

The agency is also moving forward with a substantial and controversial cut in 340B drug reimbursements, though not quite to the extent that it initially proposed.

The final rule adjusts these drug payments down from the current bar of average sales price (ASP) plus 6% to ASP minus 28%, as opposed to the proposed 33.4% below ASP. To make the reimbursement change budget neutral as required by statute, OPPS payments for non-drug services will be boosted by 7.5%.

CMS said it tweaked the proposed 340B reimbursement cut based on 340B ceiling price data from the Health Resources and Services Administration, with the baseline reductions informed by a drug acquisition cost survey it conducted earlier this year. Those changes will reduce Original Medicare drug payments by $4.35 billion and the drug payments from beneficiaries by $1.1 billion (or $900 on average per beneficiary who receives an affected drug from a hospital outpatient department) in the policy’s first year, CMS said.

The rate adjustment did little to calm hospital industry groups who’d railed against the 340B reimbursement changes as a threat to safety-net care and a gift to for-profit hospitals that do not participate in the subsidy program but would benefit from the budget-neutral offset. The American Hospital Association, the Association of American Medical Colleges and America’s Essential Hospitals each condemned the finalized policy in reaction statements, with the latter two issuing more forceful rebukes.

“CMS has based its reimbursement to 340B hospitals on the results of a hasty and faulty survey, failing to follow the clear requirements of the Medicare statute,” AAMC Chief Health Care Officer Jonathan Jaffery, M.D., said. “The drug payment cuts disproportionately affect and target non-profit and public hospitals serving low-income patients, while redistributing billions of dollars to hospitals that do not share the same safety-net and academic missions. These cuts threaten access to critical services made possible by the 340B program and will lead to worse health outcomes for patients.”

“Rather than strengthening and preserving safety net funding, CMS is instead prioritizing hospitals that do not serve the safety net and nodding to the pharmaceutical industry's interests,” AEH’s statement reads. “These cuts are landing at a time when essential hospitals and their mission are more important than ever, as millions of people risk losing their Medicaid coverage in the coming months.”

The Federation of American Hospitals, which represents for-profit hospitals that do not participate in 340B, struck a different tone, calling the rulemaking “a win for patients, taxpayers and the hospitals that communities depend on.”

“By bringing Medicare drug payments more in line with actual costs, this reform will lower seniors’ out-of-pocket expenses and redirect billions in Medicare dollars where they’re needed most—supporting access to care for patients served by small and rural hospitals across the country,” Charlene MacDonald, the group’s president and CEO, said in a statement. “We applaud the Administration for advancing a smarter, fairer approach to Medicare payment.”

Additionally, CMS finalized an increase in an annual OPPS conversion factor to non-drug items and services, speeding up the agency’s effort to offset a prior $7.8 billion recoupment to 340B hospitals it was forced to make after previously reducing 340B rates contrary to law. But while this accelerated payback—often described by non-340B hospitals as a clawback—was proposed as an increase from 0.5% to 3%, the final rule brings the conversion factor to 2%.

AHA Senior Vice President of Public Policy Analysis and Development Ashley Thompson described both 340B policy changes together as a “continued assault” on the subsidy program that “will further stress hospitals’ ability to care for their patients.” AAMC’s Jaffery said the combined policies’ impact “will be devastating to patients,” and that his group “remains committed to pursuing all available avenues to reverse these payment cuts.”

Further rankling the hospital industry was the final rule’s continued push toward site-neutral payment policy and a phase-out of the inpatient-only list.

For the former, the agency will apply Physician Fee Schedule payment rates for some imaging services to off-campus provider-based departments. In line with the proposed rule, CMS said it expects the plan to trigger first-year reductions in Medicare Part B expenditures and beneficiary cost-sharing obligations of $260 million and $70 million, while also reducing a market incentive for provider consolidation.

Meanwhile, CMS finalized its proposal to remove 647 services from the inpatient-only list, allowing them to be delivered and paid for in the lower-cost outpatient setting.

“Both policies are misguided,” AHA’s Thompson said, “and overlook the unique role hospital outpatient departments play in delivering care to patients in communities nationwide. Hospital outpatient departments treat sicker and more complex groups of patients than independent physician practices, including those with higher rates of disability and who are more likely to come from low-income and rural communities.”

CMS’ fact sheet on the 1,270-page annual rule also noted various changes to quality reporting programs for hospital outpatient departments and ASCs, a proposal to require prior authorization for eight botulinum toxin injection codes CMS said it has seen a jump in claim volume, and new permission for accreditation organizations to assess hospitals’ compliance with EMTALA (the Emergency Medical Treatment and Labor Act of 1986).

“CMS is committed to spending taxpayer funds wisely and efficiently, so [that] America’s seniors can continue getting the care they need,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in a release. “By right-sizing payments and rooting out waste, today’s reforms will drive affordability in Medicare for current beneficiaries while ensuring it will still be there to serve their children and grandchildren.”

Injective (INJ), PEARL and Zcash (ZEC) Surge as Crypto Traders Return to Altcoins

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Injective (INJ), PEARL, and Zcash (ZEC) are gaining attention as crypto traders return to altcoins, with renewed momentum across decentralized finance, AI infrastructure Layer-2s, and privacy-focused assets.

The latest rallies highlight how investors are looking beyond Bitcoin and major alts for opportunities in different crypto sectors. Injective offers exposure to on-chain finance, Pearl Research is tied to decentralized computing, while Zcash continues to attract interest around privacy.

The rally also comes as we watch upcoming project developments and look for fresh narratives beyond established coins.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Injective (INJ) remains focused on decentralized finance and on-chain trading infrastructure. Its proof-of-stake network allows holders to delegate tokens to validators and earn staking rewards. Injective has also attracted institutional attention through investment products tied to its native token, adding another potential catalyst for market interest.

Pearl (PEARL) has built its narrative around AI and blockchain infrastructure, with a network designed to support decentralized computing through its Proof-of-Useful-Work model.

Zcash (ZEC), meanwhile, is drawing attention as a privacy-focused crypto. Its shielded transaction features offer users a way to protect transaction details, while its proof-of-work consensus means holders cannot earn native staking rewards like INJ holders.

The three tokens represent different areas of the crypto market, but their recent gains point to renewed appetite for altcoin exposure.

The altcoin rebound is also putting Bitcoin scaling projects back in the spotlight, particularly those seeking to expand Bitcoin’s role beyond a store of value. Bitcoin Hyper is developing exactly this: a Bitcoin Layer 2 designed to support faster transactions and smart-contract functionality through the Solana Virtual Machine.

The project’s HYPER token is currently offered through a presale priced at just $0.0136, with the presale scheduled to end on November 26, 2026. The project’s official website reports that the sale has raised more than $33.2 million.

Something magical is happening above the Bitcoin ecosystem. ⚡️🔥https://t.co/VNG0P4FWNQ pic.twitter.com/iOEqyn0aGq

That puts Bitcoin Hyper’s presale in its final weeks, with buyers facing a stated deadline before the planned token launch. The current price is a presale price, however, and does not guarantee the token’s eventual exchange value.

Bitcoin Hyper also offers 35% annual percentage yield for users who stake HYPER during the presale. This rate is substantially higher than the reward rate available through Injective crypto staking, which varies with network conditions and validator commissions. The comparison is notable for traders weighing potential staking rewards across crypto assets.

Zcash and PEARL do not offer native proof-of-stake rewards in the same way, as their networks use proof-of-work consensus.

Bitcoin Hyper’s fundraising also provides a point of comparison with other crypto projects. Its $33.2 million presale total exceeds the $25 million seed round raised by Zcash Open Development Lab, a company building products around the Zcash ecosystem.

Injective is close, having raised $40 million in a private token sale, while Pearl Research Labs has reported a larger equity funding total.

Bitcoin Hyper’s approaching November 26 deadline is becoming a key part of its presale story. The project aims to combine Bitcoin’s liquidity with faster transactions and programmable applications, using an SVM-based Layer 2 architecture. Its roadmap includes a bridge, transaction processing, and settlement back to Bitcoin.

The testnet is being developed separately from the planned token launch, with full mainnet functionality remaining a future milestone.

For us watching the altcoin rebound, HYPER offers exposure to a different narrative: expanding Bitcoin into a network for decentralized applications and on-chain finance.

With the November 26 deadline approaching, Bitcoin Hyper is positioning itself as one of the presale projects seeking to convert renewed crypto market interest into demand for a Bitcoin-focused ecosystem.

« Zidane, il ne s'est jamais pris pour Zidane » : Les vérités sur le parcours de légende de Zinédine Zidane

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

La trajectoire d'entraîneur de Zinédine Zidane continue de fasciner le monde du football. Dans sa biographie intitulée Zidane, la légende, parue le 14 octobre 2026 chez City Éditions, le journaliste Arnaud Tulipier décrypte le parcours singulier du technicien tricolore et revient sur son humilité restée intacte malgré la gloire.

Ancien journaliste pendant 25 ans au sein des rédactions de L'Équipe et de France Football, Arnaud Tulipier a choisi d'aborder la carrière du Français sous un angle inédit. Préfacé par Luis Fernandez et enrichi par le témoignage de Jean Fernandez, le premier entraîneur professionnel de Zinédine Zidane à l'AS Cannes, l'ouvrage s'attarde sur sa simplicité. En effet, malgré une notoriété planétaire, l'ancien joueur a toujours préservé une discrétion totale. « Zidane, je dirais qu'il ne s'est jamais pris pour Zidane... Alors que c'est le Français peut-être le plus connu au monde ! Il a tous les outils en main pour faire quelque chose de grand », a confié l'auteur dans un entretien accordé à Tendance Ouest.

Pourtant, à l'issue de sa carrière de joueur en 2006, la plupart des observateurs et de ses proches n'imaginaient pas un tel destin sur un banc de touche. « Tous les gens que j'ai interrogés et tous les témoignages que j'ai compulsés disaient la même chose après sa carrière de joueur : 'il ne sera plus dans le foot, il ne sera jamais entraîneur, il n'a pas la personnalité pour...' », a rappelé Arnaud Tulipier. Un doute rapidement balayé.

Pour lancer cette nouvelle carrière, Zinédine Zidane a choisi la voie de l'exigence et du travail. Loin de profiter de son immense statut pour brûler les étapes, le technicien a catégoriquement refusé le moindre passe-droit. Il a ainsi validé ses diplômes d'entraîneur en France avant de rejoindre les bancs du CDES de Limoges pour y suivre un cursus de manager.

Par la suite, Zinédine Zidane a méthodiquement gravi tous les échelons au Real Madrid. D'abord nommé ambassadeur du club puis conseiller spécial du président, il est devenu adjoint de José Mourinho avant de diriger l'équipe réserve. Une étape fondatrice avant son intronisation comme entraîneur principal.

Sur le banc du Real Madrid entre 2016 et 2021, Zinédine Zidane a rapidement confirmé ses compétences en remportant un total de 11 trophées majeurs. Le technicien français s'est même payé le luxe d'entrer dans l'histoire du football en devenant le premier entraîneur à remporter trois Ligues des champions d'affilée. Une performance inédite. D'ailleurs, cette réussite s'explique avant tout par sa gestion humaine et son autorité naturelle auprès du vestiaire. « Quand Zidane entre dans une pièce, tout le monde est en admiration, il y a une aura. Zidane, il n'a jamais cessé d'être un joueur et il a une autorité naturelle », souligne le journaliste.

Trump Media Moves $344 Million in Bitcoin to Crypto.com as TRUMP Hits a Record Low: How Are Trump’s Crypto Ventures Doing?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

TRUMP crypto check: Trump Media just moved its BTC collateral to Crypto.com weeks before a $1B note deadline. Is it selling?

TRUMP crypto ventures came under fresh pressure after Trump Media moved 4,259 BTC, about $344 million, out of wallets holding its convertible-note collateral and into a Crypto.com-linked address. This happened a day after the TRUMP memecoin hit a record low. Lookonchain flagged the transfer at 03:00 UTC. The blockchain shows two transactions, of 2,140.04 BTC and 2,119.31 BTC, confirmed in block 974,613 at 02:17 UTC.

The move lands in a bad week for every Trump-linked asset. After Tuesday’s US midterm elections went the Democrats’ way, the TRUMP token, World Liberty Financial’s WLFI and Trump Media’s stock all fell, even as Bitcoin climbed back above $80,000.

The question traders are asking is simple: are the Trump businesses selling their Bitcoin? The blockchain answers part of it.

Trump Media transferred 4,259 $BTC ($343.67M) to https://t.co/uXlOLXEpBd 40 minutes ago.https://t.co/b8FnEmGpPh pic.twitter.com/A3yEfMmhuf

— Lookonchain (@lookonchain) November 5, 2026

DISCOVER: Bitcoin Back Above $80,000 as $468 Million of Shorts Get Wiped Out: Can BTC Clear Its Pre-Hack High?

The amount is the first clue. Trump Media’s second-quarter 10-Q says 4,260.73 BTC served as collateral for its convertible notes, with restrictions lifted “no later than” May 29, 2028. Thursday’s two transfers add up to 4,259.35 BTC, and both sending wallets are now empty.

The address the coins went to is not new either. The same Crypto.com-linked deposit address, 34jvUbzJ…yMy, took 2,649.9 BTC from Trump Media wallets on May 22 and 2,628.3 BTC on August 1. Each time, the coins were swept into one large wallet that holds about 17,330 BTC from many users.

Once coins sit in a pooled exchange wallet, the blockchain can no longer show whether they were sold, lent or simply held in custody. Trump Media has not commented on Thursday’s move. The timing is what traders are focused on.

A registration statement filed on November 2 says holders of Trump Media’s $1 billion convertible notes can force the company to buy them back for cash on November 30, 2026, and that it “could be required to pay up to approximately $1.0 billion”.

The same filing lists ways to raise the money, including to “sell our bitcoin or other digital asset holdings to fund such repurchases.” At about $80,600, the 4,259 BTC that just moved are worth roughly a third of that bill.

The company’s Bitcoin pile was already shrinking. Trump Media held 9,477.16 BTC on June 30, down from 9,542.16 at the end of 2025, and booked a $360.6 million loss on its digital assets in the first half of the year.

American Bitcoin, the miner co-founded by Eric Trump, is adding coins rather than dumping them. It reported on November 4 that its reserve grew to about 8,427 BTC at the end of September, from 8,002 in June.

It mined about 891 BTC in the quarter but only kept about 425 of them, because it “sold a portion of Bitcoin production to cover operating costs.” That is a miner paying its bills, not a treasury exit.

Almost every listed or tradable Trump business is near its lows, with one exception: the stablecoin.

Token prices are from Binance at 09:00 UTC on Thursday, with peaks and USD1 supply from CoinGecko. Stock prices are Wednesday’s Nasdaq closes.

The TRUMP token took the biggest hit. It fell from $1.396 to a record low of $1.219 in a single hour from 17:00 UTC on Wednesday, and it now trades 98.3% below its $73.43 peak from January 2025.

Trump Media closed at $4.49 on Wednesday, just above its 52-week low of $4.46. Besides the note deadline, it is asking shareholders to approve an all-stock merger with fusion energy company TAE Technologies at a special meeting on December 14.

American Bitcoin’s numbers look better than its stock. It reported third-quarter net income of about $126 million, mostly from a $197 million gain on its Bitcoin, yet the shares are down 26% in a month.

USD1 is the only piece holding steady, trading at its $1 peg with $3.70 billion in circulation.

TRUMP traded at $1.26 on Thursday morning, far below its 20-day EMA at $1.63 and its 50-day EMA at $1.78. It has no earlier trading history below Wednesday’s $1.219 low.

TRUMPUSDT 1D chart. Source: TradingViewTradingView

A bounce in TRUMP would need buyers who see value in Trump-branded assets after the vote. For now, the cheapest signal to watch is the onchain one: if more Trump Media coins head to the same Crypto.com address before November 30, TRUMP crypto sentiment is likely to stay heavy.

The 10 Best Rimfire Scopes of 2026: Budget to Precision Optics Tested

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 10:12 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

A rimfire rifle used to be the next step up from a Red Ryder. You’d grab it to plink at the range, teach a new shooter, or shoot a few squirrels. But rimfire rifles have evolved, and so has the entire hobby of shooting them. It’s now completely normal to expect a rifle to shoot 1-inch groups at 100 yards and a range session to stretch from 25 to 300 yards.

Scopes have kept pace with the rifles. You might find a basic 3-9x on a stock 10/22, but you won’t find one on someone’s RimX build. Today’s rimfire shooter wants close-focusing adjustable parallax, a first-focal-plane reticle, and plenty of elevation travel to reach well beyond 100 yards.

I selected the best rimfire scopes with those demands in mind, but I haven’t forgotten the utilitarian and budget friendly scopes. Here are my top picks from my testing and our optics tests.

I use rimfires for hunting, fun shooting and occasionally competition. I’d typically need at least two scopes to cover those pastimes: a handy 3-9x for hunting and a 3-15x for shooting small targets at extended ranges. But the Nightforce NX6 2-12×42 F1 can take on all of those uses, and do them well.

We tested the entire NX6 line at Outdoor Life’s 2026 optics test, and I was thoroughly impressed with the 5-30x56mm F1’s ability to ring steel at 300 yards and focus down to 11 yards. That scope is a bit big for my typical rimfire uses. The 3-18x is a highly versatile option that Outdoor Life Editor-in-Chief, Alex Robinson, recently picked as the best high-end scope for deer hunting, but it’s still a bit heavy for my taste. The 2-12x is my pick for the ultimate rimfire scope because it’s ideally suited for small-game hunting, plinking and shooting steel beyond 100 yards. While it’s not the scope you want for hitting tiny targets at long range, it has enough magnification to hit rimfire-sized targets around 100 yards, and I routinely shoot my KYL rimfire rack with it at 50 yards.

The scope’s attributes extend beyond its versatility. It has decent glass, an incredibly easy-to-use zero stop, close focusing and excellent tracking. Some might find the FC-MRx reticle too busy, but I like it for fast holdovers and its reference points for wind holds.

I currently have the NX6 2-12×42 on a CZ 457 American with a Proof Research carbon-fiber barrel. The rifle is a hammer and very light, and the NX6 is right at home on it.

Read Next: CZ 457 American Review: Quarter-Inch Groups Right Off the Rack

If you’re looking for to compete in NRL22, there are several excellent scopes to choose from. But the Razor HD Gen III 6-36×56 is my overall best pick. It focuses down to 10 yards, has 36 MRAD of elevation adjustment, and plenty of magnification for shooting matchsticks and other tiny targets. The new VMR-5 reticle is a nice clean reticle that’s ideal for people who prefer dialing and you can still get the EBR-7 reticle if you prefer holdovers.

Here’s what optics editor, Andrew McKean, had to say about the Razor HD Gen III after our optics test:

Vortex might have been satisfied with simply upgrading the reticle inside its flagship precision scope, which has won enough PRS and NRL matches to qualify as a class leader. But with the new Gen III version of the venerable Razor HD, Vortex has created a new turret system that is precise, smart, and innovative.

The heart of the turret update is what Vortex calls its L-TEC+ zero system in which relaxing a single set screw allows users to make fast and precise turret adjustments. The system allows zeros to be established between the turrets’ .1 MRAD clicks, meaning that shooters no longer have to be satisfied with turrets set a half click above or below their true zero, a fudge factor that is more problematic the farther you shoot.

The operation takes a bit of getting used to. Tester John B. Snow, thinking the external slot on the head of the turret was designed to accept a beefy screwdriver bit, reefed on our sample, breaking the pot-metal, before reading the instructions and learning that once the retaining screw is loosened, the turret moves easily with only fingertip tension. Our other rifle-range revelation: The creepy caterpillar-looking grub that emerges from the base of the turret is not an embedded chrysalis but rather a smart revolution indicator that becomes fully emergent on the third full rotation of the elevation turret.

For the smart turret updates, the Vortex wins our Innovation Award, but that’s hardly the only update to this excellent scope. The new EBR-7D reticle is a marvel of efficient simplicity. Built around a .03 MRAD red-illuminated center dot with open aiming cross, the reticle’s elevation steps are in .2 MRAD hatches, and windage steps in .2 MRAD dots. We would like to see slightly bolder indexing, as the reticle references were hard to see below about 11-power.

The image delivered by the Razor HD is one of the best in the class, and we liked the turret indexing and smart and useful pull-to-turn illumination control. With a very close parallax adjustment and the infinitely adjustable zero, this would make an excellent rimfire competition scope.

Vortex is understandably proud of this scope, but with a retail price of around $4,000, we think it’s pretty pricey compared with its peer group. — Andrew McKean

There’s not much innovative or surprising here, just a basic duplex reticle inside a straight-ahead scope with decent turrets and a durable build. Backed by an excellent warranty.

If simplicity is the pathway to versatility, then the Vortex Crossfire II is at home on a small-game bolt rifle or a fast-shooting AR. It might not have the chops for precision work, but the duplex reticle is fast and reliable. The controls are a little mushy, but the eye relief and eye box are both forgiving and the fixed parallax makes it a great plinking scope. — Andrew McKean

Another in a long line of solid, bright Hawke scopes. This sub-$450 Vantage 30 is an upscale sibling to the Vortex Crossfire HD 6-18×50. Both sport useful second focal-plane BDC reticles with center-dot illumination. But the Hawke brings a little more sophistication to its controls, with exposed pull-to-turn turrets, immersive field of view, and a floating center-dot aiming point that lights up in either green or red.

Optics are good but not great. The illumination is good but we wish it was more intense to boost visibility in bright sunlight. And turret tracking started to slip a bit at the very end of its elevation travel.

But the Hawke impressed us with its smooth, tactile controls, and the BDC reticle is fast, with MOA-based references that correspond to the drop of standard center-fire loads. With a 50-yard zero with our standard 40-grain CCI .22 LR ammunition, we easily stretched the reticle to 300 yards by dialing 35 MOA of elevation and holding another 10 MOA. That’s impressive range for the scope-and-rimfire combo.

“It’s a simple, straightforward hunting scope that’s priced about right,” says tester Dale Manning. Enough said. — Andrew McKean

One of the surprises of the test is how testers universally fell in love with this little, versatile scope. On the surface, it seems like an oddball. Less than 10 inches long, it requires a cantilever mount to fit most rifle receivers, and it looks more like an LPVO than a serious precision scope.

But once you get behind the GPO Centuri Compact, a whole world is revealed through the excellent milling reticle. At lower magnifications with the illumination at its highest intensity, the reticle functions as a point-and-shoot red-dot sight. But at higher magnifications, starting about 10x, the reticle’s precision references become visible. A series of .1 MRAD hashes enables pinpoint aiming. For shooters who prefer to dial, the big exposed turrets pull to turn and move with authority. A smart revolution indicator pops up on the elevation turret when shooters enter the second revolution.

​If you’d rather tune the reticle to your specific load, GPO offers a free custom ballistic turret with purchase. That’s an additional value for a scope that’s already priced fairly at about $650.

Testers debated the best use cases for the Centuri Compact. Some felt it’s best suited to an AR carbine. Others wanted it on their big-woods whitetail rifle, and still others felt it would be an excellent scope for stalking elk in dark timber. That latter use is enabled with a smart illumination that lights up the floating center dot and inverted horseshoe. The red illumination is infinitely adjustable thanks to a rheostat control.

​The eyebox is stingy at higher magnifications, which forces shooters to have perfect alignment. But that’s a minor miss. The scope garnered enough pluses to finish in the top third of the field of scopes that are much more squarely configured for hunting.— Andrew McKean

The Busnell R7 won the editor’s choice award for being the best hunting scope of the year. While it’s an excellent big-game optic, it’s also right at home on a rimfire rifle. Here’s McKean’s take on this excellent piece of glass:

With this big, bright, and surprisingly sophisticated R7, Bushnell is restoring its relevance as a first-order hunting optic brand. The first focal-plane scope has an open tree-style reticle that’s anchored by an illuminated segmented circle and floating center dot that enables precision work as well as fast holdover and wind-informed shots at game.

For hunters and target shooters who would rather dial, the pull-to-turn elevation turret is nicely indexed and moves with positivity. The windage dial is capped.

Bushnell has invested heavily in the R7 line, which is the best of the Good/Better/Best product-line hierarchy that started with the R3 a couple years ago and continued with the R5 last year. The company has four different configurations of the R7, released just this month: a 1-8×24 LPVO, a 2-10×42, and a 3-15×50 in addition to the 4-20×50. The lower magnification scopes are available in second-plane reticles, but the two higher-mag versions come in either first- or second-plane reticles and either MIL or MOA, giving buyers a crazy number of variations on the platform.

We tested the second-plane MOA version of the 4-20×50, but for its versatility, performance on a wide range of targets and distances, and its flawless handling, we named the FFP R7 not only best Bushnell in the test, but the best overall hunting scope in the field of 25 excellent competitors. They include Nightforce’s new NX6, Leupold’s VX-4HD, and Zeiss’s new V3 families, along with some excellent submissions from Vortex, Trijicon, and Burris.

For its overall performance, the Bushnell R7 4-20×50 is this year’s Editor’s Choice winner as the top hunting scope of 2026.

The scope stacked points with a combination of good ED Prime glass, clear indexing on its controls, tight and positive turret and focus controls, and an illuminated Hybrid Dot Tree reticle that splits the difference between a precision competition and a hunting reticle. Its zero stop, which we’re glad to see on this type scope, is a 6-screw affair that’s a hassle to set, but works wonderfully as a solid backstop on elevation turret travel.

We worried about the durability of the R7, but it passed our inaugural drop test without skipping a beat. We dropped the scope three times from three feet on concrete covered by a 2-inch pad, and the Bushnell retained its zero.

Of course, our test teased out some deficiencies. The reticle references aren’t especially visible until about 7x, and I’d like to see one or two more intensity steps to the center-dot and segmented circle illumination to make them pop in daylight.

But if you’re looking for a rimfire or long-range competition scope that you can easily transition into a big-game hunt, this is a wonderful choice. The price, at about $950, is fair for the versatility and fine handling of this true crossover scope. — Andrew McKean

Set-it-and-forget-it hunters will appreciate this solid, honest scope for its lack of adornments. No illumination. A simple plex reticle in the second focal plane. It’s like the Fullfield mentioned above but with better glass and controls.

But hunters who are practiced at ranging a target, then dialing an aiming solution based on ballistic solvers will go crazy for this new configuration of the venerable Burris Veracity. The elevation knob is exposed, nicely indexed, and ready to dial. A little too ready, as it doesn’t lock. For some OL testers, that’s a problem, as an exposed turret can easily get pushed off zero given the hard knocks of the field.

But Burris has that figured out, too, with a zero stop that ensures a return to zero. It’s a tedious 6-screw affair to set, but once the zero stop is established, it is a rock-solid baseline for dialing.

​Given the wave of hunting scopes containing first-plane reticles with busy windage and elevation references and illumination modules, this iteration of the Veracity is refreshing. But it also forces users into either dialing for distance or sticking with the single aiming point at the crosshairs. We’d rather see the 3-15×44 Veracity with the illuminated RC-MOA (Rapid Cross) reticle that gives hunters the option of dialing or using reticle references to hold for elevation and wind.

We quibble a bit at the $1,000 price for such a stripped-down scope, but the turret tracking was among the best in the field, and Burris’s “Knob Synergy” elevation dial is tight, positive, and the low-profile build won’t hang up in the field. All in, the 3-15×44 version of the Veracity is a welcome addition to the 2.5-12×42 and 5-25×50 versions. — Andrew McKean

Combining simple controls and a modest but versatile magnification range, this durable scope is perfect for just about any .22 or .17, whether for small-game hunting, plinking, or most precision target work. It has good glass, repeatable controls, and enough holdover references on the vertical stadia that, with some practice, you can extend your range out to 100 yards and longer. At close to $300, it’s priced in the meaty middle of the market and can easily transition from a first-gun optic to just about any other rifle, whether a rimfire or not.

Here’s a rimfire scope for just about any purpose. This outwardly pedestrian optic has a lot going on between a surprisingly precise second-plane reticle, bright glass, and fun indexing on the nicely tactile turrets. The best thing you can say about any scope is that it is consistent, and the VX-Freedom delivers bullets with pleasing reliability. It’s a good rabbit and squirrel scope. Still, the MOA-based references on the vertical crosshair make it a decent option for engaging distant targets, which means you don’t have to invest in a ponderously big first-plane target scope to take titles at NRL22 competitions. Lastly, the Leupold rimfire scope brings the company’s good glass and an excellent warranty.— Andrew McKean

Tract’s 22 FIRE series has a full series of scopes for .22 and .17 shooters, but this AO model (it stands for Adjustable Objective) is the most versatile scope in the line from the direct-to-consumer brand. The duplex reticle features a fine crosshair with a .25 MOA center dot, which allows for full view of the target and precise bullet placement, which competitive bullseye target shooters will appreciate.

The adjustable objective enables precise focus for targets inside 10 yards all the way out to infinity, a feature you don’t often see on rimfire-specific scopes. The objective focus is a little clunky compared with the side parallax of most modern scopes, but it works well and further enables precise shooting. At about $275 it’s not a cheap scope, but the glass is good, the controls are some of the best in the business, and the precision it allows also makes it a killer squirrel scope. — Andrew McKean

The Burris Eliminator 6 is the easy button for long-range shooting. It ranges, calculates ballistics, and tells you where to aim. Here’s what shooting editor, John B. Snow, said about the Eliminator 6 in his precision scope buying guide:

This is the most innovative scope in this roundup. Burris has long been a category leader in scopes with integrated rangefinders, and Eliminator 6 is the newest iteration of that tech. Once the scope has been fed a ballistic profile and zeroed — neither process is overly complicated — it delivers impressive point-click-and-shoot capability.

The second focal plane reticle contains a multitude of LED illumination points. When you range your target — which is done via a large button on the left side of the scope or through a remote Bluetooth switch — one of those dots lights up indicating your hold. It is remarkably idiot-proof.

I know we live in an age of technological marvels, but the fact that you can roll through the scope’s magnification range and the aiming dot will slide up and down the crosshair continuously displaying the correct hold is remarkable.

Targets beyond 1,000 yards can become difficult to laze, and you’ll likely need to fine-tune your ballistic profile to keep the scope’s solutions spot-on. But within 800 yards this system couldn’t be quicker or easier to employ. — John B. Snow

Your first consideration if you’re in the market for a rimfire rifle scope is how well it will perform with the short-range capabilities of the caliber. You probably don’t need a ton of magnification or reticle references if all you want is to shoot tin cans or 50-meter bullseye targets. But the world of rimfire shooting is evolving to look a lot like long-distance center-fire precision shooting, with all sorts of precision rimfire competitions and games that put a premium on both short-range and long-range precision. For backyard plinkers, you probably just want a simple scope with a duplex reticle, but for the latter, you’re going to want a first-plane reticle with lots of turret adjustment. Then there are the pure small-game scopes. These are lower-magnification optics with just enough reticle references to get you on game and maybe make longer shots, head-shooting squirrels, for instance.

For small-game hunting, I want a scope that gets on target fast, has a reticle that “pops” from the background, but has enough references for me to place bullets precisely out to about 75 or 100 yards. In this collection, the standout hunting scope is Bushnell’s Trophy Quick Acquisition (QA), a 1-6×24 marvel that points quickly, has an illuminated center dot for low-light work, and enough holdover references for longer shots. Others worth a look: Vortex’s Crossfire II, and Tract’s 22 FIRE. At the other end of the spectrum is EOTECH’s expensive 1-10×24 Vudu, which doubles as a marvelous AR optic. Or consider Leupold’s VX-Freedom. Its simple duplex reticle is fast and reliable.

I like lower-power scopes for most rimfire work, whether that’s hunting or target shooting. A 2-7-power or even 1-8-power will give you all the magnification you need for most purposes. But if you’re interested in the precision rimfire competitions, then you’re going to want more magnification to really see the nuances of distant targets. For those times, I’d go with a 5-20-power or 4-16-power range, and make sure that precision target scopes also have manual focus—often called parallax—to bring targets of whatever distance into crisp focus.

The price of rimfire scopes ranges all the way from about $100 up to nearly $2,000. There are problems with both ends of that spectrum, just as there are great deals as long as you know what attributes you want and how you’ll use the scope. Starting at the lower end of the price spectrum, I’d be a little leery of super-cheap scopes, simply because they are probably made with substandard components and may not be as durable as higher-priced models. If you spend under $100 on a scope, make sure it has a good warranty that will cover any breakage or malfunctions. But you can buy a lot of scope for under $200. Scopes in this roundup include Bushnell’s Trophy QA and Vortex’s Crossfire II, and both the Leupold VX-Freedom and Tract’s 22 FIRE AO are both priced around $300 and offer excellent durability, utility, and warranties. Precision rimfire scopes can be especially expensive, largely because their precision controls and components are built to a higher standard. Still, the Sightmark Presidio, priced under $400, and the Vortex Strike Eagle are both worth looking at for budget precision options. On the other end of the spectrum, both the EOTECH Vudu and Blackhound Emerge offer tons of precision for rimfire shooters, but have all the attributes to transition once you move up to center-fire precision target shooting.

Somehow, the optics industry has squeezed all the fun out of rimfire shooting. Instead of cheap, reliable, and utilitarian rifles, we now have fancy chassis rifles with all the attributes of their big-bore brethren. And prices to match. We’ve complicated what was once the most fun and simple type of shooting into rigorous competition. That’s all great if you want to make .22 shooting into a serious thing, but don’t forget that rimfires are the gateway for most shooters and hunters into our community, and the scopes and rifles that are the best for new shooters are often the most simple and cheap. So read this review with a little bit of skepticism. There are optics for every stage in a shooter’s evolution, but maybe pay special attention to those fun, simple, gateway scopes.

Scott Einsmann is Outdoor Life’s gear editor. He oversees the gear team’s editors and writers who are subject matter experts in bows, knives, hunting, fishing, backpacking, and more. He lives in Richmond, Virginia with his wife and two bird dogs.

Andrew McKean is Outdoor Life’s hunting and conservation editor, drilling into issues that affect wildlife, wildlands, and the people who care about them. He’s also OL’s optics editor, helping readers to make informed buying decisions.
