Take a Break this Winter. Warren Miller’s ‘Days Off’ Is Coming to a City Near You.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After 77 years of iconic ski films, Warren Miller is asking a question never posed before: What do the world’s greatest skiers and snowboarders do on a day off?

Days Off celebrates skiing for skiing’s sake. This fall, Warren Miller’s new narrator and host, Katie Burrell, is taking audiences from backcountry laps to heli drops and the après in between with the world’s best to find out what they do when they slow down and rediscover the art of the day off.

Tickets are on sale now and run throughout North America until late December. Find a screening near you here.

Marty Shaffer, founder of backcountry ski company CaPow, knows how to take a day off. The film features his annual trip to Blanket Park Chalet in Revelstoke, British Columbia. Shaffer’s parents bought the helicopter-access-only A-frame cabin in 1986, a month before he was born.

“Back then, it was a bunch of granola munchers,” Shaffer told Outside. “For years growing up, it was hard to get people to ski with me.”

Now, he and his wife run guide-curated backcountry ski trips with their two young boys. Burrell was also Shaffer’s first employee, and bringing her back to the chalet held a special place in the film.

“The story and the segment are super meaningful for my family because we filmed, and then ten days later, we had our second kid,” Shaffer said. “It’s special to me because it’s a story that we were able to tell at a very specific time in our life, and the magic of our family.”

Shaffer invited Burrell on the friends-and-family trip, and when she accepted the Warren Miller hosting position, she asked if she could bring a film crew

“It’s a special time for us to be up there with family,” Shaffer said. “Working as a guide and having a small business, this is one of our only times to really enjoy Blanket with our family and away from all the chaos.”

Shaffer has been watching Warren Miller films for ages, and said this year’s film reflects on the importance of relationships, slowing down, taking time off, and getting outside.

The film includes seven other segments shot across three continents, taking audiences from the remote heli-skiing terrain of Alaska and high-alpine mountaineering of Chamonix, France, to legendary powder of Hokkaido, Japan, and the pristine slopes of Deer Valley, Utah. The journey continues through the heart of Canada’s best ski destinations—Whistler, Pemberton, and Banff—capturing the sport’s raw spirit through backcountry snowmobiling, skijoring, and endless alpine pursuit.

Burrell joins athletes and industry professionals on their days off—unscripted runs, backcountry lodges, and local traditions that live just beneath the ski industry’s surface.

Hundreds of showings are still on deck, spanning the U.S. and Canada. Some events will feature giveaways, others will have surprise guests, and all will build community and stoke excitement around our favorite season: winter

Other featured athletes include Sam Kuch, Craig Murray, Jess Kimura, Marie-France Roy, Christina Lustenberger, Guillaume “Gee” Pierrel, Craig McMorris, Sebastian Judge, Mark Sollors, Truth Smith, Court “Blackbird” Larabee, Mason Mashon, Kaylin Richardson, Ted Ligety, Chuck and Claudia Mumford, Pep Fujas, Sam Mitchell, Marty Schaffer, Kate VanHee, and Himari Takamori, Hinano Sakamoto, Shizuku Hanada, among others.

Warren Miller’s Days Off is presented with support from Mammut, Deer Valley, Brooks, REI Co-op, Swany, Silverton Mountain, DryBrew, and in production with Outside Studios.

These 6 Nordic-Cycle Cabins Make Recovery the Ultimate Focus

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

(Photo: Left: WestWind Cabins cold plunge tub/Brian Reid - WestWind Cabins Owner; Middle: Taking a dip in the cold plunge at Desert of Maine Campground & Unwind Nordic Spa/Ben Sarle; Right: Cool plunge at Frontcountry Salida/Daylene Wilson Photo; Design: Ayana Underwood/Canva)

There’s something so invigorating about unwinding in a hot, dry sauna, then plunging into an ice-cold tub or pool. While such contrast therapy is offered at high-end spas and popular among wellness and outdoor enthusiasts, the practice has long been a staple of Nordic living and recovery.

To save you the search for the ultimate Nordic cycle retreat, I researched the most beautiful cabins in nature, decked out with saunas and cold plunges. Before sharing them with you, though, let’s take a beat to understand the scientific allure of contrast therapy—the risks associated with it.

“Nordic cycling improves circulation by shifting rapidly from heat to cold, flushing metabolic waste and lactic acid from your tired muscles,” Harry Pino, PhD, an exercise physiologist at Princeton Longevity Center, told Outside. Princeton Longevity Center offers comprehensive diagnostic health testing and preventative care. “It triggers a release of norepinephrine, dopamine, and endorphins, increasing alertness, focus, and a sense of well-being,” he said.

Additionally, Pino said cold exposure specifically suppresses inflammatory pathways, which is particularly beneficial for athletes and fitness enthusiasts because it speeds up recovery. Moreover, research has linked regular sauna routines to a lower risk of dying from cardiovascular disease.

Although the Nordic cycle has a reputation for being wildly beneficial, it’s not without risk. Pino said potential sauna side effects may include intensified itching, heat discomfort, lightheadedness, and low blood pressure.  “It is also possible for blood pressure to rise or fall depending on the person’s cardiovascular health,” Pino said. Cold plunges, on the other hand, can cause cardiovascular stress, hypothermia with prolonged exposure, and cold shock, which can be deadly. “It is advised for people with pre-existing health conditions to consult a doctor before taking the plunge,” he said.

If you’re ready for the ultimate recovery experience, keep reading for six stunning sauna-and-cold plunge cabins worth booking to explore the wellness trend for yourself.

You’ll feel immersed in nature, even though you’re five minutes from downtown and ten minutes from the world-renowned Homer Spit (a.k.a., the halibut fishing capital of the world), in one of four architecturally unique cabins that sleep four to six guests. On the grounds, enjoy the inclusive spa, where a six-person hot tub, cedar-clad wood-fired sauna, and cold plunge tub await you and your adventure buddies. For a taste of Alaskan adventure, wander around Kachemak Bay. The cabins overlook the famed waterway, known for world-class recreation from running trails and paddle sports to fishing opportunities and whale watching.

Enjoy front-row access to some of Colorado’s most exhilarating mountain recreation in Salida. Suitable for two to four guests, these thoughtfully designed wellness cabins make returning from nearby Monarch Mountain just as enjoyable as the slopeside adventures themselves. Each cabin, called a boutique micro-cabin, includes a private cedar barrel sauna, cold plunge, outdoor stargazing canopy, and fire pit, making it ideal for winter après-ski. You’ll also be able to relax after hiking, climbing, and mountain biking, or paddling, rafting, and fly fishing along the neighboring Arkansas Headwaters Recreation Area, which is also in Salida and just a 20-minute drive away. A word to the wise: pack layers; temperatures can vary greatly between highs and lows, with fall and winter weather dipping into the teens and rising into the mid-seventies.

Idaho is an under-the-radar playground for outdoor enthusiasts, often getting overlooked for Montana, Wyoming, and Colorado. One stay at these cabins, which boast Japanese-inspired beds and accommodate up to six people, and are just a short 30-minute drive from Yellowstone National Park’s West entrance, and you’ll want to return to explore the area again and again. In addition to private wood-fired hot tubs at each cabin, the retreat offers a communal Nordic spa, complete with a sauna and cold plunges. When you’re not cycling between contrast therapies or cedar-soaking beneath the galaxy, consider a scenic stroll or top-notch fly fishing along Henry’s Fork of the Snake River. If dry adventure is more your thing, head over to Harriman State Park, where more than 22 miles of hiking, biking, horseback riding, and Nordic skiing trails are at your disposal.

Hidden in a pocket of lush New England forest, this woodsy retreat feels miles from civilization. In reality, it sits mere minutes from downtown Freeport, Maine, and 20 minutes from Portland. The campground’s A-frame cabins and stargazing domes (sleeping two to six guests) offer the best of both worlds: full immersion in nature and easy access to the area’s best recreation, shopping, and dining. You’ll also be just ten minutes from L.L. Bean’s flagship store, where you can watch live fish swim in the indoor ponds and take a photo next to the iconic duck boot sculpture, which stands roughly 17 feet tall. The on-site Unwind Nordic Spa (featuring a sauna, invigorating cold plunge—never more than 60 degrees Fahrenheit—with a waterfall, and a salt-water stone hot tub that hits 104 degrees Fahrenheit) draws wellness lovers to unwind beneath the Northern Pines. You can plan your stay at the campground, and you can book spa appointments now.

The adjacent but separate tourist attraction, also called Desert of Maine, lures visitors with its intriguing sand dunes and gemstone mining for kids. Though this tourist attraction is closed for the season, it reopens in May 2027, so you can start planning your next spring adventure now. For a more mountainous excursion, meander over to Hedgehog Mountain Trailhead or Bradbury Mountain State Park to explore miles of multi-use trails. Or, if you have a bit more time to spare and are craving an unforgettable coastal climb, Acadia National Park is just under three hours away.

Planning a Glacier National Park getaway? This forested retreat, with cabins sleeping up to four guests, is about an hour from the West Glacier entrance and offers wellness amenities that make the drive well worth it. Beyond the cozy, vintage-inspired Montana cabin decor, this Western woodland escape boasts a unique Nordic circuit, with a custom-built, forest-view Finnish cedar sauna and a natural, spring-fed stone group cold plunge pool. Just beware: local trout call the plunge pond home. Outside of camp, nature is calling. Nearby Flathead Lake is a popular spot for kayaking, paddleboarding, and swimming, while Blacktail Mountain Ski Area is great for intermediate to advanced skiers (only roughly 15 percent of slopes are greens).

There’s so much to see and do in Shenandoah National Park that booking an overnight seems only obvious. This hidden, Scandi-style A-frame, suitable for up to four people and located just over an hour from Washington, D.C., invites guests to relax and recharge among the forest, then sweat and plunge beneath the stars. A few can’t-miss sights (and flavors) in the area include White Oak Canyon, with its six waterfalls ranging from 25 to 86 feet; Old Rag, Shenandoah’s most popular and strenuous hike; and The Apple House, a Linden institution frying up the most delicious apple butter cinnamon donuts you’ve ever had. (No, really: the roadside restaurant has drawn crowds since 1963, and three generations of my family have been loyalists—it’s that good.)

CZ Pardon Faces New Democrats Scrutiny A Week After $6.3B Binance Hack

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Democrats are continuing their investigation into Changpeng Zhao, known as CZ, pardon as Binance faces renewed scrutiny following a $6.34 billion security breach. The Democrats inquiry into CZ pardon is bringing fresh attention to Binance’s founder, who says he no longer manages the exchange but remains one of its most vocal public defenders.

The October 29 Binance hack targeted internal wallet-signing systems, triggering unauthorized transfers across several blockchain networks. Binance says it will absorb the financial impact, while CZ has publicly defended the exchange and reassured users.

The incident has added another layer to questions surrounding Zhao’s relationship with Binance. Despite stepping down as CEO after his 2023 guilty plea, he remains closely associated with the company and continues to comment on major developments.

Democrats have been investigating if political connections, financial relationships, or lobbying influenced President Donald Trump’s decision to pardon Zhao in October 2025.

In May, lawmakers sent letters to 17 clemency recipients, including Zhao, seeking information about their pardon applications and any involvement from lobbyists, intermediaries, or political allies. The inquiry later expanded to federal offices involved in the clemency process.

The scrutiny intensified after Binance’s security breach. On October 30, House Financial Services Committee Ranking Member Maxine Waters pointed to Binance’s reported ties to the Trump family’s World Liberty Financial, the SEC’s decision to drop its case against Binance and CZ, and Trump’s pardon.

Zhao pleaded guilty in 2023 to failing to maintain an effective anti-money-laundering program at Binance. He stepped down as CEO and served four months in prison before receiving the presidential pardon.

The Democrats investigation focuses on how clemency was obtained and whether financial or political relationships played a role in CZ pardon. Binance’s hack has now placed the exchange’s security and accountability in the same spotlight.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Binance’s November 3 incident update put the affected assets at $6.34 billion. The attacker gained unauthorized access to an internal wallet-signing service and initiated transfers outside the exchange’s normal withdrawal process.

The attack moved assets across several networks, including XRP, Solana, Optimism, Bitcoin and Ethereum. Binance said approximately $2.85 billion had been frozen or intercepted, while 98% of affected token assets had been replenished by October 31.

The breach targeted Binance’s internal wallet infrastructure rather than BNB Chain’s consensus mechanism. No BNB Chain consensus exploit was reported, distinguishing the exchange-side compromise from an attack on the blockchain itself.

why was no BNB hacked?why did the hacker wait for the USDC and USDT to be frozen (nearly 30 mins) instead of swapping for ETH?why slowly TWAP out of memecoins over multiple hours instead of just insta nukingwhy was a single compromised laptop from a devops guy able to drain…

CZ has remained highly visible throughout the response. After Elon Musk appeared to mock the incident online, Zhao asked him to stop trolling and referenced Binance’s SpaceX investment, which he said was worth more than $1 billion.

The exchange’s co-CEO, Yi He, later said Binance’s revenue was sufficient to cover the losses and that it did not need to sell the SpaceX stake. CZ later clarified that his comments were not an announcement of a planned sale.

Elon, have a lot of respect for you, but bad time to troll. Please stop.Our SPCX shares (from our $500m investment in your Twitter takeover) are worth more than a billion.Would appreciate if you show a little support. Thanks!

The exchange says it has reserved more than enough funds to cover the losses and is continuing to strengthen the affected systems. Its investigation remains ongoing, with independent cybersecurity reviews planned.

This episode illustrates how closely his public profile remains tied to Binance, even after leaving the CEO position. He continues to defend the company, address users, and respond to major developments involving its finances and operations.

As Democrats pursue questions surrounding CZ pardon, Binance is working to restore confidence after one of the largest reported crypto security incidents. The two developments have brought renewed attention to Zhao’s political connections, his ongoing influence, and the exchange’s ability to protect customer assets.

Just as the Rut Kicks Off, 2 Popular Climbing Stick Companies Issued Recalls

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 9:55 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Two hunting equipment manufacturers, Latitude Outdoors and Hunt Arsenal, have issued immediate stop-use notices following reports of potentially dangerous equipment failures. Both companies are investigating the reported issues and advising customers to discontinue use until further notice. Here’s what you need to know.

Latitude Outdoors has issued an immediate stop-use notice for its popular Carbon Synergy Climbing Sticks after receiving reports of steps moving, slipping, or detaching from the climbing stick posts. This system of climbing sticks was popular among public-land hunters and even won our review of the best climbing sticks (before these safety concerns came to light).

A company representative says the company identified the bottom step as the point of failure after several customer complaints.

Latitude acquired Treestyx LLC in late 2025 and subsequently introduced the Carbon Synergy Climbing Stick. The product retained the original Treestyx design and construction while incorporating a new knotless attachment method.

According to Latitude, earlier versions of the product met industry testing requirements, and the Carbon Synergy Climbing Sticks underwent additional testing and recertification before their release. Warranty claims involving the step-to-post connection have raised concerns about the product’s reliability, however, ultimately resulting in its recall.

Although the company said the reported failures represent a small percentage of products in use, it is advising all customers to stop using Carbon Synergy Climbing Sticks immediately.

Latitude is offering full refunds to customers, regardless of where the climbing sticks were purchased. Customers who purchased directly through Latitude can initiate a return through the company’s online portal and receive a prepaid shipping label. Refunds are also available for Carbon Synergy Illuminators and Carbon Synergy Stealth Strip Kits purchased directly from Latitude.

Customers who purchased Carbon Synergy Climbing Sticks through authorized retailers should return them to the original retailer for a refund.

Retailers have also been instructed to discontinue sales and remove affected products from inventory. For more information, visit Latitude Outdoors’ return portal and see their official recall notice.

Obviously, this poses a major problem for deer hunters who are in the field now. Some social media commenters even claimed that they were going to take the risk and use the recalled sticks anyway, since they were already on their hunting trip.

Another popular saddle hunting company, Hunt Arsenal, has also issued an immediate stop-use notice for its 2026 Maxx Step Double and Triple Aiders. According to Hunt Arsenal, the attachment cord connecting the aider to the climbing stick may fray, separate or break during use, creating a serious fall hazard. The stop-use notice advises any customer using 2026 Maxx Step Double or Triple Aiders to stop using them immediately, even if the attachment cord appears undamaged.

While the company works on a solution, Hunt Arsenal advises customers to set affected products aside and not return or discard them until further instructions are provided. Hunt Arsenal expects to provide additional information early in the week of Nov. 9, including instructions for customers who purchased directly from the company or through authorized dealers. Hunters unsure whether their aider is a 2026 model should also discontinue use until they can confirm its production year.

The company said reports received so far involve only 2026 production models, and it is investigating a possible manufacturing issue that may have damaged the cord in some units. The Maxx Step Single Aider is not affected by the notice.

Updates will be available through Hunt Arsenal’s website and social media.

Slavia Prague 1-2 Arsenal: Madueke and Gyokeres fire Gunners to Champions League victory

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal moved up to third in the Champions League table after beating Slavia Prague 2-1 at the Fortuna Arena.

Noni Madueke opened the scoring after nine minutes, finishing calmly after Viktor Gyokeres sent him through on goal.

The Swedish striker then doubled Arsenal’s advantage with an excellent finish in the 38th minute. However, a much stronger second-half performance from Slavia made for an uncomfortable finish.

Wiktor Nowak scored a late goal to put pressure on the Gunners. They held on to take all three points.

Mikel Arteta made six changes from the Liverpool win on Sunday. William Saliba returns to the squad after his back injury.

The defender was left on the bench, with Ezri Konsa playing alongside Gabriel Magalhães in central defence.

Arsenal started brightly and took control of the game when they won the long ball forward. Gyokeres beat his man and found Madueke with a clever through ball.

The winger used his pace to get into the penalty area before sliding his finish through goalkeeper Nazar Domchak’s legs.

Arsenal continued to create chances, with Mikel Merino going close on several occasions. Domchak also produced an important save to deny the midfielder from close range.

Merino eventually helped Arsenal extend their lead. After winning possession in the Slavia half, Declan Rice found Gyokeres, who exchanged passes with the Spaniard before curling a superb finish into the far corner.

The visitors could have added a third before the break. Gabriel’s header from a corner was cleared off the line.

Arsenal appeared to be in control at half-time.

But Slavia upped the tempo and started to create more space, forcing Arsenal into a defensive stance. Early on, Oskar Kubiak fired a long-range shot, forcing David Raya to push it away.

Arteta got increasingly frustrated as Arsenal struggled to hold on to the ball and dominate the midfield.

They tried to keep pressing, but Arsenal struggled to create much at the other end. Madueke did get into a good position to shoot from an angle but didn’t have enough power to cause any problems for Domchak.

Slavia were denied a goal when Gabriel headed the ball away from the goal line. Raya made a brilliant stop to keep the shot out while the penalty area was crowded.

The Arsenal keeper made another stunning save as Kubiak powered a header from a cross. Raya deflected the ball onto the post to keep his team ahead by two goals.

Slavia scored their breakthrough goal in the 90th minute after David Jurasek played a low cross from the left. Nowak was able to run into the box to score past Raya.

It created a nervy few minutes of added time as Arsenal had to defend their lead.

Slavia could find no way back into the game as Stepan Chaloupek headed wide and Mikulas Konecny threatened in the dying moments, but his effort lacked confidence as Raya made light work of saving.

Arsenal secured their third win in their last four Champions League games to move to 10 points and into third place in the group stage.

The Gunners have now gone unbeaten in their last ten Champions League away games to set a new club record.

However, Arteta will be looking for a much better display from his side when they resume action in the Premier League against Hull City on Saturday.

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Bitcoin Back Above $80,000 as $468 Million of Shorts Get Wiped Out: Can BTC Clear Its Pre-Hack High?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin news: BTC closed above $80,000 for the first time since the Binance hack as $468M of shorts were wiped out. Will it hold?

Bitcoin news turned to a short squeeze on Wednesday, November 4, as BTC closed above $80,000 for the first time since the October 29 Binance hack. A jump at the US stock market open forced traders to close $468.5 million of short bets. Bitcoin trades at $80,550, up 2.7% in 24 hours. ETH USD price did better, rising 4.4% to $2,427.

The move came in two steps. BTC went from $78,321 at 14:30 UTC, when New York opened, to $79,559 within 15 minutes, then pushed to $81,196 by 15:15 UTC. Earlier the same day, it had dropped to $75,736, so Wednesday’s range was more than 7%.

Most of the traders caught out were betting on a fall. That matters, because a rally fed by forced short covering can fade once the buying stops.

Bitcoin is resilient. pic.twitter.com/qUHWw2uwnt

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 4, 2026

Discover: Cboe and Citadel Sue to Stop US Stock Perps Hours After the CFTC Approved Them: Why Hyperliquid’s $5 Billion-a-Day Market Has the Most at Stake

CoinGlass counted $715.6 million of crypto futures liquidations over 24 hours, and 65.5% of them, or $468.5 million, were shorts. Some 99,879 traders were liquidated.

Bitcoin led with $267.1 million liquidated, and 93.4% of that was shorts. Ether followed with $197.5 million, 58.1% of it shorts. Binance handled 39.3% of all liquidations. The largest single order went the other way. A $38 million ETH long was closed on HTX at $2,429.33, close to where ETH trades now.

On OKX, BTC futures open interest rose to $3.88 billion during the jump, then fell to $3.74 billion by midnight UTC. A drop like that suggests traders closed positions rather than opened new ones.

The crowd has not turned bullish. The OKX ratio of BTC accounts holding longs to those holding shorts fell from 1.84 at 11:00 UTC on Wednesday to 1.52 on Thursday morning, so more accounts are now betting against the rally.

BTC sits right on top of its 20-day EMA at $80,006, and its 50-day EMA at $80,166, so the $80,000 area is now both a round number and the first support.

The $81,971 level is where BTC traded before news of the Binance hack broke. Reclaiming it would erase the hack-day drop on the chart.

Options traders are calm about it. Deribit’s DVOL index, which tracks expected Bitcoin volatility over 30 days, eased to 36.9 from 38 on Wednesday.

The next Bitcoin news to watch is whether spot buyers step in now that the short covering has cooled. Without them, $80,000 is the line that decides whether this was a breakout or only a squeeze.

52 points, 24 rebonds et 8 contres : Victor Wembanyama signe un record historique !

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Victor Wembanyama a signé une prestation monumentale cette nuit sur les parquets de NBA. Avec deux nouveaux records personnels à 52 points et 24 rebonds, plus 8 contres, le pivot tricolore a porté les San Antonio Spurs vers la victoire face aux Dallas Mavericks (112-111). Une soirée historique.

Dans la nuit du mercredi 4 au jeudi 5 novembre 2026, au Frost Bank Center de San Antonio, le Français a écœuré les Dallas Mavericks lors d'une victoire étriquée (112-111). En seulement 34 minutes de jeu, Victor Wembanyama a effacé son précédent record en carrière qui culminait à 50 points depuis novembre 2024 face aux Washington Wizards. Un exploit retentissant.

La portée de cette performance s'avère inédite depuis près de 60 ans en NBA : le pivot devient le premier joueur de l'histoire à cumuler au moins 50 points, 20 rebonds et 8 contres sur un match depuis l'introduction du contre en 1973-1974. D'ailleurs, depuis cette saison, seul Bob McAdoo avait réussi à compiler au moins 50 points, 20 rebonds et 5 contres, en 1974.

Le pivot a imposé son rythme d'entrée avec 10 des 12 premiers points de San Antonio, bouclant le premier quart-temps avec 15 points, 8 rebonds, 2 contres, 2 interceptions et une passe. Dans le deuxième quart-temps, il a réussi 3 contres en l'espace de 13 secondes sur une même possession adverse. À la pause, ses 29 points, 15 rebonds et 5 contres constituent une première depuis l'instauration du suivi action par action en 1996-1997.

Agressif dans la raquette, Victor Wembanyama a terminé avec un 16/28 aux tirs, dont 14/18 dans la peinture et 1/8 à trois points. Sur la ligne, son 19/23 aux lancers francs établit son record personnel de tentatives en NBA. Selon les calculs d'ESPN, San Antonio a d'ailleurs affiché un différentiel de +23 points avec son pivot sur le parquet, contre -22 lors de ses passages sur le banc des remplaçants.

Interrogé après la rencontre sur sa marge de progression offensive et sa domination dans la raquette, le joueur a livré une analyse lucide de sa prestation : « Je peux encore faire plus. Mes tirs ne rentraient pas et cela m’a contraint à adopter un style de jeu très fatigant. Je peux le faire, la preuve est là. Mais je peux être bien meilleur lorsque mes tirs rentreront », a confié Victor Wembanyama, avant d'ajouter : « J’ai été très bon dans un secteur de mon jeu en attaque et très mauvais dans l’autre. Je m’attends donc à être bien meilleur lorsque les deux fonctionneront en même temps ».

Pourtant diminué ces derniers jours, l'intérieur tricolore a retrouvé toutes ses jambes pour faire basculer la rencontre. « Il y avait peut-être quelque chose de spécial, mais il s’agissait surtout de comprendre ce qui se passait. Parfois, on a un temps d’avance. Je ne me sentais pas très bien ces derniers temps, je suis malade depuis un moment. Aujourd’hui, j’ai retrouvé mes jambes. Cela devrait donc arriver plus souvent », a expliqué le pivot, avant de saluer l'acharnement collectif : « Je pense que nous avons fait le bon choix sur la plupart des possessions. C’était surtout une question d’acharnement. Nous en voulions davantage ce soir ».

La fin de match a été très disputée. À 24 secondes de la sirène, Victor Wembanyama a inscrit le lancer franc de la gagne pour porter le score à 112-111, avant que le meneur de Dallas Kyrie Irving ne manque son tir en pivot à trois secondes du terme. Dylan Harper a épaulé le pivot avec 21 points tandis que Stephon Castle a compilé 17 points et 10 rebonds. Chez les Mavericks, le Français Zaccharie Risacher a été limité à 2 points et 2 passes en 18 minutes.

San Antonio aligne ainsi une sixième victoire consécutive pour afficher un bilan de six succès et deux défaites, grimpant à la cinquième place de la conférence Ouest. Les San Antonio Spurs disputeront leur prochain match le vendredi 6 novembre 2026 face aux Sacramento Kings.

S.J. Bennett on the brilliance of 1960s British mysteries and thrillers

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

My first date with my husband, twenty-five years ago, was a series of tests. Do you like Blackadder, and agree that series 4 is the best? Are you a fan of Bladerunner? The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy? And, very importantly, did you read Len Deighton as a teenager? Luckily for our future son, we both passed.

Deighton’s unpredictable protagonists, and his propulsive writing style, were a revelation to sixteen year-old me. I always think of his heroes in cars, on the run from something awful, on the way to something worse.

Growing up in the 80s, I was a fan of the classics, of P.G. Wodehouse and Jilly Cooper. People behaved badly, very badly sometimes, but their role in the moral universe was clear. Then, in books written at around the time I was born, as the dust was finally settling from WW II, I found myself in the cynical, murky world of double agents and double-crossing, inspired in part by the Noir of the 30s, but updated to the Cold War: we can’t trust the men in charge, and the world may be about to end.

In the historical series that I’m writing featuring the late Queen, I’ve reached the mid-60s. The last book was set at the height of the space race in 1961, and the new one takes place in the Caribbean in 1966. I think of this as a sunny highlight: the Swinging Sixties, The Beatles, Carnaby Street, the young Rolling Stones….

But research quickly reminded me that it was the time of Vietnam, the Bay of Pigs, the call for independence among many of the UK’s old colonies, of drugs and disillusion, fear and paranoia. This is the world that Deighton explored in Funeral in Berlin.

His unnamed protagonist, played by Michael Caine and given the name Harry Palmer in the films (because it was the most boring one they could jointly come up with), had already had two outings, in The IPCRESS File and Horse Under Water. Both are a bit bonkers. Funeral in Berlin, published in 1964, was where Deighton really got into his stride.

The Berlin Wall had only gone up three years before. I lived there in the 80s, when the Wall went round the back of our garden and there was a Russian sentry post a few meters beyond. People were still dying in the attempt to get across, but by then, the Wall had its own mythology.

When Deighton was writing, the weird cruelty of streets and even houses being cut in half by barbed wire and concrete was still fresh, and bled into the text. I equally loved John Le Carré for his more cerebral evocation of the times, but Deighton’s working-class anti-hero, at odds with the Establishment and unwilling to look away, was the one who really got me into the genre.

How does Frederick Forsyth get away with the plot of The Day of the Jackal? Set in 1962, in the aftermath of the Algerian war of independence, it’s a story in which we already know the outcome—the assassin must surely fail to kill Charles de Gaulle, who didn’t die until 1970—and yet we’re gripped as we watch him regularly outwit the authorities, who are no slouches in tracking him down.

Inserting a false narrative into real events is what I do with my own series, so I’m with him as Forsyth tightly interweaves the two. I always used to feel that the final-final twist, where a character we think is dead shows up unexpectedly, didn’t quite make sense. However, when I recently relistened to the audiobook version, I realized that it had been set up that way all along. It makes the reader completely rethink the middle section, paying tribute to the value of dogged police work, even when the odds against success seem overwhelming.

The novel also pits a middle-class hero, the police deputy commissioner Claude Lebel, against a dismissive, upper-class superior. The moment when Lebel gets his revenge is very satisfying. Like Deighton, Forsyth was no fan of the old-school ruling classes, although he had an obvious respect for methodical assassins.

James Bond (another iconic spy name designed to be as boring as possible) evolved gradually as a character. He was based in part on old Etonian Ian Fleming himself, partly on some of the men he encountered in wartime naval intelligence, and later on the director of Dr No on screen, the refined and sophisticated Terence Young, It was Young who gave him his sharp tailoring from Anthony Sinclair, his taste for martinis and elegant way with cigarettes.

But the first man to play him, with a panther-like grace that got him the part, grew up in a tenement in Edinburgh, leaving home to work as a bricklayer, lorry driver and coffin polisher, among other things. Would Bond have been as interesting if he hadn’t had the magnetism that Sean Connery brought to the role? At a time of great social mobility, he was a stand-out star.

Connery didn’t play Bond in the film of On Her Majesty’s Secret Service. By 1969, he’d given up on the carnival of publicity around his life. But he was filming Dr No near Fleming’s Goldeneye estate in 1962 when Fleming wrote the novel.

Fleming probably had Connery (and those Anthony Sinclair suits) in mind for the Bond who pretends to be Sir Hilary Bray from the London College of Arms. In the end, though, the part went to the actor George Lazenby, who made the somewhat premature decision that James Bond would be a busted flush in the 1970s, and turned down a chance to return.

In this book, Blofeld is the villain, and the plot moves on from classic Cold War territory to a more generalized tech-driven anxiety, in which a bio-war is about to be unleashed by unsuspecting female debutantes. It’s not the most feminist of plots, but I love the novel for Bond’s female companion, Tracy di Vicenzo, the only woman in the Bond canon—apart from Vesper Lynd—who I think holds her own in a three-dimensional way.

Played to perfection by Diana Rigg onscreen, Tracy is smart, well-connected (her father is a very serious crime boss), reckless, resourceful, quick-thinking and athletic. Having created a woman who’s a worthy match for Bond, the series obviously couldn’t keep her. The ending of is so tragic and unexpected that it’s still gut-wrenching, even now.

On the whole, I’d say we don’t think of Agatha Christie when we think of the 1960s. The perennial films and TV series conjure inter-war fashions, Miss Marple’s Edwardian correctness, old-fashioned country houses, and the Art Deco smartness of Hercule Poirot.

But her success spanned decades. The Pale Horse, published in 1961, was partly inspired by the very popular novels by Dennis Wheatley, which often featured the supernatural. In Christie’s version we have foggy nights, witches, deserted country lanes and several predicted deaths in seemingly-impossible circumstances.

One of the secrets of Christie’s success was how cutting-edge she was with her murder methods. She loved to know about new poisons and the latest gadgets. Think of the role a Dictaphone plays in one of her most famous early books—Dictaphone as a company had only been created three years before it was published in the 1920s.

And true to the Christie formula, the solution to the Pale Horse mystery contains an up-to-date knowledge of chemistry. It works both as an out-of-time, spooky novel and a contemporary, 1960s plot.

In my books, Queen Elizabeth II occasionally has a Dick Francis novel at her bedside, and she was a big fan in real life. As a jockey, Francis was closely connected with the royal family. He was riding the Queen Mother’s horse, Devon Loch, towards victory in the 1956 Grand National, when the horse inexplicably fell. This was when he retired to become a writer.

Dead Cert, which came out in 1962, was his first novel. It’s centered around great horses falling in important races, and Francis’s visceral first-hand experience lights up the page. But there’s more to his books than horses. As Britain’s post-war economy took off, he branched out with his wife, Mary, to research a wide range of industries. exploring the glamour and danger of his age.

Like Deighton’s, I read his books in the 1980s, when Proof grabbed my attention. I’m drawn to Francis’s vulnerable heroes, so gentle with horses, so outrageously lucky or unlucky with women, so tough at withstanding injuries of all kinds (as the author was) and beatings from his sadistic villains. I have to read through half-closed eyelids, but still, I have to read on.

One of the things I like about Dick Francis’ career is that it took time to take off. Like Ian Fleming’s, his early books sold well enough, but there weren’t any fireworks. It was only as the series progressed that publicity grew, and he became the crimewriting juggernaut his estate still is today. There’s a lesson for publishers there, perhaps: have faith in your writers. Stick with them. Fandom comes with time and repetition.

It’s a lesson another British spy novelist embodies. Mick Herron’s Slough House books languished in the also-rans for years, until a US publisher spotted them and they got a new lease of life.

This autumn, season 6 of Slow Horses returns triumphantly to our screens. It contains many of the tropes of its 1960s predecessors: downtrodden heroes, defying the odds and besting upper class antagonists; realistically dingy streets and double-crossing; up-to-date technology and an overarching sense of anxiety. We can’t trust the men in charge, and the world may be about to end. Plus ça change.

S. J. Bennett was born in Yorkshire, England, and lives in London. An army child, she grew up traveling around the world. Her first novel was published when she was forty-two, after a varied career and lots of procrastination. She is the award-winning author of several books for children and teaches and podcasts about writing.

Kennedy Cole on writing about race through invasion horror and parasitism

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This essay will break down the themes of racism, homophobia, and existing as other that I touch on in There Used to Be People Here. I’ll expand on my thought process behind Samuel’s dilemma with the taur, and explore some of the complex moments that happen in the book (as much I can without spoilers). Imposter syndrome and belonging are two of Samuel’s biggest struggles; I plan to touch on that in aspects of my own life, and as an issue Black people deal with as a whole.

Foreign occupation of the body has always been a particularly terrifying horror element to me. The idea of losing control of not just our mind, but our being—autonomy, duality, and person—sounds so far out there that to some, it might be too impossible to fear.

Of course, it is an impossibility, in some ways. Very rarely, if ever, does one quite literally lose control of themselves to an outside source. In some ways, though, the idea is very real.

Take imposter syndrome, for example, the intrusive thought that you as an individual don’t belong in a room you’ve been—presumably—welcomed into. It happens to all of us, doesn’t it? We question our ability to be, wonder whether or not we truly belong in a space we, more often than not, do.

We allow, then, ourselves to change. We lose ourselves to paranoia. We become invisible in a space that originally valued us because of our unique ability to shine.

In my debut, There Used to Be People Here, Samuel Barkley is the only Black detective in a small police department in Mississippi, 1978. He works alone by choice, but also by default. He consistently questions his own worth, mostly as a result of the constant stares and pressured environment created by the officers around him, but also as a result of his own deeply buried trauma. Things shift when he’s introduced to a new case—a strange murder that starts in a gay bar—and an odd new partner who he has uncertain feelings about, Harvey Wilkins.

And of course Samuel wonders, do I deserve this?

As a Black woman, I’ve often asked this question myself whenever I find I’ve been given new, and most often exciting, opportunities. I attended a predominantly white K-12 school from Kindergarten until graduation, so trust me when I say I’ve had my fair share of stares and pressure and deeply buried trauma.

I’ve also had privilege: the privilege to learn at a private institution, to explore creative writing in a passionate environment, to research subjects I love and be encouraged to grow. At every turn, though, I found myself questioning my (what I now know is natural) ability to do so.

I found myself questioning my Blackness, because many other people around me—both Black and white—were doing the same. Was I truly talented, or was it a case of borrowed opportunity? Were my dreams tangible, or faraway wonders? Could I truly achieve all that I set my mind to, or was I just the only Black girl pretending to be white?

The taur—the worm-like, parasitic hive-mind antagonizing Wenton, Mississippi in my debut—propose this question in the most fitting way possible: by occupying the body. They feed on Samuel’s twisted desire to be invisible by creating an environment where this is true. To Samuel, the taur’s true motivations are murky, but their plans are clear: to make everyone, everywhere the same.

The idea of sameness can be beguiling to those of us who face discrimination because of our innate, unchangeable qualities. Samuel’s position as a detective in the Wenton Police Department is earned, and yet his role as the only Black man in the room pushes him to call his sense of self into question, much like I did as the only Black girl in my grade for thirteen years. It’s easy to wish ourselves away, to wonder whether homogeneity would erase the questions and stares altogether and allow our inner selves to shine.

And though in theory it would, our innate, unchangeable qualities are lost as a result. Our Blackness—which, even in a room full of people who are not, does matter—becomes invisible. We forfeit our bodies. We allow the parasite within us to win.

Throughout the story, Samuel’s autonomy does become forfeit, not because of a parasitic entity infecting him, but because of his own fear of existing as other. His queerness, which he discovers through his unique partnership with Harvey, is something he attempts to fold away for later. Over time, Samuel’s work as a detective shifts from a childhood dream to a tool determining his self worth. Working his way up through the ranks is his only true talent. And to Samuel, his Blackness is only an obstacle.

When I first began writing There Used to Be People Here, my identity had yet to be fully determined. In many ways, it still is wholly undiscovered. I’m a twenty-three year old woman publishing my very first book, preparing to be married to the love of my life in just under a year, and still looking for a job in my field long after graduating with a bachelor’s degree. I’m living this life for the first time, taking it one day at a time just like everyone else.

My sense of self, though, is much stronger than it was when I started my debut. I can think of no better reason for this than Samuel.

I parallel my novel’s main character in more ways than one. What frightens him frightens me. As Samuel works through his fear of unifomity—and the temptation to try it for himself—I was finding my voice as a queer Black writer.

To be blunt, writing about my race has been difficult for me. Up until this point, I’d never centered any of my stories around a Black main character, let alone a Black man. I wouldn’t realize it until I was well into drafting, but writing There Used to Be People Here challenged everything I’d learned about what it means to be a writer. My own work forced me to face the pattern I’d perpetuated, and in the context of the story, forced Samuel to look inward and face himself.

Now, I can look back and map Samuel’s journey alongside my own. Our Blackness—our innate, unchangeable worth—destines us to be othered. So, we shine not in spite of it, but because of it. We choose diversity, not because we welcome discrimination, but because our differences are a direct reflection of our autonomy. We reject the parasite and embrace each other, because our worth is determined by the depth of our love.

Kennedy Cole is a speculative author with a BFA in Creative Writing and a Certificate in Publishing. Born in North Carolina, she lives by the beach with her partner and enjoys playing games, puzzling, sipping matcha, and collecting tattoos. Her creative portfolio includes an array of published fiction, poetry, and nonfiction with Oakland Arts Review, Carolina Muse, Second Story Journal, and others. There Used to Be People Here is her first novel.

Seeking Justice in Deep South Georgia

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I had finished the third run-through of my closing argument, so I went down to the pond to see how John Petrey, my chief assistant district attorney, was doing with his portion.

I walked slowly and quietly so as not to disturb him. As I drew near, I caught sight of him standing on the dock, facing the water in his T-shirt, shorts, and Birkenstocks. Tall and slim, glasses perched on the end of his nose, hair a little disheveled, he was delivering a persuasive argument to what appeared to be an empty pond.

Across from the dock, though, I spotted the gray snout and black eyes of an alligator, the rest of the animal still and immersed in the water. The animal was watching him. Because the sun was shining from that direction, the light combined with Petrey’s terrible eyesight caused him not to see it.

That old gator was the most attentive juror he ever addressed.

We were two hundred miles from where the murder happened, living in a hunting lodge outside Albany, Georgia, preparing to close the biggest case I would ever try.

Eighteen months earlier, on December 15, 2000, during a cold and rainy night in Georgia, Derwin Brown, the sheriff-elect of DeKalb County (one of the largest metropolitan Atlanta counties), was ambushed and shot twelve times in his own driveway. The killing sent shockwaves through the Atlanta area and Georgia and was reported nationally and even internationally. The preceding summer, Brown defeated the incumbent sheriff in a highly contentious campaign that was focused on corruption allegations against the sitting sheriff. An investigative reporter for a local television station exposed shocking details of the corruption. Brown was three days from being sworn in. His family was inside the house waiting for him.

Derwin’s wife ordered a custom-tailored sheriff’s uniform for the swearing-in. She had hung it inside the storm door so it would be the first thing he saw when he entered the home.

At the morgue that night, I noticed his wedding ring had been flattened. A bullet had struck it. He had apparently been holding his hand up to defend himself.

As the district attorney, I had a policy that my team would show up any time an officer was killed in the line of duty, which is how I found myself alone with the bullet-ravaged body of Sheriff-elect Brown for a few minutes. I knew that if we found the killer(s), I would be the prosecutor.

The trial was not held in DeKalb County. There had been too much media coverage. The judge granted the defense request to move the trial.

The prosecution and the defense each got to suggest three cities. One of mine was Savannah. If I was going to spend eight weeks somewhere, I thought it would be nice to be near the ocean.

People who hear “change of venue” may think of it as a technicality, or as a strategy of defense attorneys to seek out a different jury pool. It should be neither. It occurs because a judge in the county where the crime occurred determines the people who live there are not impartial, and the case needs to be moved to another county to ensure justice is delivered.

When the order came in, I thought Petrey was playing a joke on me when he announced the case would be tried in Albany, the county seat of Dougherty County, in deep South Georgia. My hometown. I have three generations buried in that sandy soil below the gnat line in Georgia.

A few years before the trial, a hundred-year flood caused the Flint River to flood over the cemetery. The caskets were hermetically sealed, so when the flood water reached them they shot up out of the ground like missiles. Fred Mays, a GBI agent and one of the most important people on my prosecution team, once told me there were two things in this world he was afraid of: rattlesnakes and dead people. During that flood, Fred’s job was to take a motorboat out over the cemetery and lasso the caskets and bring them back.

My grandmother was buried in one of the caskets that floated down the Flint River. I always wondered if Fred was the one who returned her casket to the cemetery.

The trial was held in the county courthouse where I had been sworn in as a young lawyer, and where the state of Georgia once prosecuted Martin Luther King Jr. I watched Dr. King march down Slappey Drive when I was a boy. I stood on the sidewalk with Sarah, who cared for my sister and me, and I had no idea what I was looking at. I thought it was a parade.

Forty years later, I stood in that courtroom and quoted Dr. King to a jury.

The hunting lodge was the only place we could find in the Albany area to house our team of lawyers and investigators without interruption for two months. It had a cabin that looked like the one in the old Snuffy Smith cartoon. Seventeen miles outside town, it had a tin roof, a ladder up to a sleeping room with windows on every wall, and the pond. No phones. No television. Other small cabins were also on the grounds of the hunting lodge. This is what the biggest corruption and murder prosecution in the history of the state looked like from the inside. Lawyers in shorts, isolated from the outside world, arguing to the woods.

Months before, a lawyer who had worked in my office as an assistant district attorney came to my house.

He told me not to ask him any questions. He said he knew I did not carry a gun, but I needed to start carrying one, and I needed round-the-clock security.

Lawyers are allowed to break client confidentiality when a life is in danger. My name was on a hit list.

In Georgia, as in almost all states, the district attorney is elected and answers only to the people he or she serves. (This is in contrast to the federal system, where the United States Attorneys are under the supervision of the Attorney General who serves at the pleasure of the President.) It was my decision, and my decision alone, whether to bring charges against those whom I believed were responsible for Brown’s assassination. After sharing the evidence with the most experienced lawyers on my staff and seeking their input, Tom Clegg, my chief trial assistant, said, “You are the boss. It’s your decision.” Attorney General Robert Jackson, who was later appointed to the U.S. Supreme Court, said in a speech in 1940, “The prosecutor has more control over life, liberty, and reputation than any other person in America.”

A fair question is why I decided to be the lead prosecutor on this case. First, I was a career prosecutor who had tried over 100 jury trials before being elected district attorney. In many district attorney offices, especially in urban areas, the district attorney is primarily an administrator and rarely goes to court.  Some elected district attorneys have never tried a felony case, either as a prosecutor or defense attorney. The courtroom was my sandbox.

Second, the ultimate responsibility for a conviction or an acquittal would rest on my shoulders as the district attorney. I wanted to be in charge if I was to bear the responsibility for a win or a loss. Also, I had some of the best and most experienced trial lawyers in the country assisting me. I was not alone.

Finally, this case was critically important to the people I was elected to serve. A sheriff-elect was murdered before he could take the oath of office. The voters of one of the largest urban counties in the country had their vote taken away from them before the candidate of their choosing could serve. Our investigation revealed a pattern of corruption on a scale that none of us could have imagined before the murder.

As I put pen to paper to write the story of this case in A Rainy Night in Georgia (Prometheus/Simon & Schuster), I was struck by how the issues in this case remain relevant and timely today. The strength of our criminal justice system depends on having people who are committed to public service, integrity, and adherence to the rule of law.

Petrey gave his part of the closing argument to a jury of twelve. (It was a while before he learned about the juror with a snout.)

When the verdict came in, a reporter I had known for years, the one who had helped break this case in the first place, asked me what I was going to do next. I told him I was getting a six-pack, my Jimmy Buffett CDs, and my boom box, and I was going to the beach.

We chose Bermuda. My family appreciated being out of the public eye, on an island with some of the strictest firearms laws in the world.

J.Tom Morgan served as the elected District Attorney of DeKalb County, GA, from 1992-2004. He was the first recipient from the United States to be awarded the Special Achievement Award from the International Association of Prosecutors in 2003. Prior to his time as DA, Morgan served as an Assistant District Attorney in DeKalb County for nine years and was the first prosecutor in Georgia--and one of the first in the nation--to specialize in the prosecution of crimes against children. As a nationally recognized expert on criminal justice, he has appeared on The Oprah Winfrey Show, The Today Show, CNN, Court TV, and 48 Hours, among others. Morgan has lectured to prosecutors and law enforcement officials in more than 30 states, Puerto Rico, and Europe, and served on the faculty of the National College of District Attorneys.
